(root) / algebra.bigb
algebra.bigb
= Algebra
{wiki}

= Isomorphism theorems
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Bernoulli's inequality
{c}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

Bernoulli's inequality states that $(1+x)^r\geq1+rx$ for every integer $r\geq0$ and real $x\geq-1$. In particular, $(1-x)^r\geq1-rx$ for $0\leq x\leq1$.

= Algebraic operation
{parent=Algebra}

An algebraic operation combines one or more elements of a set to produce another element.

= Binary operation
{parent=Algebraic operation}
{wiki}

A binary operation on a <set> $S$ is a <function> $S\times S\to S$.

= Associative operation
{parent=Binary operation}

A binary operation $*$ is associative when $(x*y)*z=x*(y*z)$ for every $x,y,z$.

= Semigroup
{parent=Associative operation}
{wiki}

A semigroup is a <set> equipped with an <associative operation>.

= Monoid
{parent=Semigroup}
{wiki}

A monoid is a <semigroup> with a two-sided identity element.

= Free monoid
{parent=Monoid}
{wiki}

The free monoid on an alphabet consists of all finite words in its letters, including the empty word, under concatenation. Distinct words represent distinct elements.

= Commutative monoid
{parent=Monoid}
{wiki}

A commutative monoid is a <monoid> whose operation is commutative. The natural numbers with addition form a commutative monoid that is not an <abelian group>, because positive elements have no additive inverses.

= Additive semigroup
{parent=Semigroup}

An additive semigroup is a <semigroup> whose operation is written as addition.

= Topological semigroup
{parent=Semigroup}
{wiki}

A topological semigroup is a <semigroup> with a <topological space>[topology] for which multiplication is jointly <continuous function>[continuous].

= Left-topological semigroup
{parent=Topological semigroup}

A left-topological semigroup requires only that every right translation $x\mapsto xa$ be <continuous function>[continuous].

= Ellis–Numakura lemma
{c}
{parent=Left-topological semigroup}
{wiki}

Every nonempty compact Hausdorff left-topological <semigroup> contains an <idempotent>. Choose a minimal nonempty compact subsemigroup $K$. For $a\in K$, minimality gives $Ka=K$; the nonempty compact subsemigroup $\{x\in K:xa=a\}$ is therefore all of $K$, so $a^2=a$.

= Diagonal dominance
{parent=Algebra}

A matrix is strictly diagonally dominant when $|a_{ii}|>\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|$ in every row.

\Include[lie-theory]

= Algebra over a field
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki=Algebra_over_a_field}

An algebra over a <field> $k$ is a <vector space> $A$ over $k$ equipped with a bilinear multiplication $A\times A\to A$.

= Structure constant
{title2=$c_{ij}^{\,k}$}
{parent=Algebra over a field}
{wiki}

For a basis $(e_i)$ of a finite-dimensional algebra, its structure constants are the scalars defined by
$$
e_ie_j=\sum_kc_{ij}^{\,k}e_k.
$$
They express multiplication as polynomial coordinate functions.

= Finitely generated algebra
{parent=Algebra over a field}
{wiki}

A $k$-algebra is finitely generated when finitely many elements generate it under addition, multiplication, and scalar multiplication. Equivalently, it is a quotient of a polynomial ring $k[x_1,\ldots,x_n]$.

= Artin--Tate lemma
{c}
{parent=Finitely generated algebra}
{wiki}

Let $A\subseteq B\subseteq C$ be rings. If $A$ is Noetherian, $C$ is a finitely generated $A$-algebra, and $C$ is a finite $B$-module, then $B$ is a finitely generated $A$-algebra.

= Finite generation descends along a field extension
{parent=Finitely generated algebra}

Let $L/k$ be a <field extension>. If $A\otimes_kL$ is a <finitely generated algebra> over $L$, then $A$ is finitely generated over $k$. Choose finitely many generators of $A\otimes_kL$ and collect the finitely many coefficients from $A$ occurring in them. The $k$-subalgebra $A_0$ generated by those coefficients satisfies $(A/A_0)\otimes_kL=0$, and <faithfully flat module>[faithful flatness] of $L/k$ gives $A=A_0$.

= Clifford algebra
{c}
{wiki}
{parent=Algebra over a field}

For a vector space with quadratic form $Q$, its Clifford algebra is generated by vectors subject to $v^2=Q(v)1$. In a basis this is equivalently $\gamma^\mu\gamma^\nu+\gamma^\nu\gamma^\mu=2\eta^{\mu\nu}1$.

= Gamma matrix
{title2=$\gamma^\mu$}
{parent=Clifford algebra}
{wiki=Gamma_matrices}

Gamma matrices give a matrix representation of the <Clifford algebra> of spacetime.

= Hopf algebra
{c}
{parent=Algebra over a field}
{wiki}

A Hopf algebra is a compatible algebra and coalgebra equipped with a counit and antipode. Coordinate rings of <affine algebraic groups> are commutative Hopf algebras.

= Associative algebra
{parent=Algebra over a field}
{wiki}

An associative algebra is an <algebra over a field> whose multiplication satisfies $(ab)c=a(bc)$. Unless stated otherwise, the algebras considered here have a multiplicative identity.

= Basic algebra
{parent=Associative algebra}

A finite-dimensional algebra over an algebraically closed field is basic when its semisimple quotient is a finite product of copies of the field, equivalently when every simple module occurs with multiplicity one in the semisimple quotient of the regular representation.

= Central simple algebra
{parent=Associative algebra}
{wiki}

A central simple algebra over a <field> $k$ is a finite-dimensional associative $k$-algebra that is a <simple ring> and whose center is exactly $k$.

= Artin–Wedderburn theorem
{c}
{parent=Central simple algebra}
{wiki}

Every finite-dimensional semisimple algebra is a finite product of matrix algebras over division algebras. In particular, every <central simple algebra> over $k$ is isomorphic to $M_r(D)$ for a finite-dimensional central division algebra $D$ over $k$. The integer $r$ and the isomorphism class of $D$ are unique.

Indeed, decompose the right regular module of a finite-dimensional semisimple algebra $A$ as
$$
A_A\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^sS_i^{n_i},
$$
where the $S_i$ are pairwise nonisomorphic <simple modules>. The <Schur lemma> makes $D_i=\operatorname{End}_A(S_i)$ a <division ring> and gives $\operatorname{Hom}_A(S_i,S_j)=0$ for $i\ne j$. Left multiplication identifies
$$
A\cong\operatorname{End}_A(A_A)
\cong\prod_{i=1}^sM_{n_i}(D_i).
$$
Conversely, the column module over $M_n(D)$ is simple and the regular module is a direct sum of $n$ copies of it, so finite products of such matrix rings are semisimple. The simple-module decomposition also proves uniqueness up to permuting the factors.

= Tensor product of central simple algebras
{parent=Central simple algebra}

The <tensor product> over $k$ of two central simple $k$-algebras is central simple. After extending scalars to an algebraic closure, both factors and their tensor product become full matrix algebras; faithful flatness then descends simplicity and the center.

= Brauer group
{c}
{title2=$\operatorname{Br}(k)$}
{parent=Central simple algebra}
{wiki}

The Brauer group of a field $k$ consists of <Morita equivalence> classes of central simple $k$-algebras. Multiplication is induced by tensor product, the identity is $[k]$, and the inverse of $[A]$ is the class of the opposite algebra $A^{\mathrm{op}}$.

= Crossed-product algebra of a Galois extension
{title2=$A(L,G,\phi)$}
{parent=Brauer group}

For a finite Galois extension $L/k$, Galois group $G$, and normalized two-cocycle $\phi:G\times G\to L^\times$, the crossed-product algebra is
$$
A(L,G,\phi)=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in G}Lu_\sigma,
$$
with $u_\sigma a=\sigma(a)u_\sigma$ and $u_\sigma u_\tau=\phi(\sigma,\tau)u_{\sigma\tau}$. It is a central simple $k$-algebra split by $L$.

= Cohomological construction of a Brauer class
{parent=Crossed-product algebra of a Galois extension}

Cohomologous normalized two-cocycles define isomorphic crossed-product algebras: replacing $\phi$ by the coboundary associated with a one-cochain rescales the basis elements $u_\sigma$. Consequently the crossed-product construction induces a map
$$
H^2(G,L^\times)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Br}(k).
$$

= Center of an associative algebra
{title2=$Z(A)$}
{parent=Associative algebra}

The center of an associative algebra $A$ is
$$
Z(A)=\{z\in A:za=az\text{ for every }a\in A\}.
$$

= Group algebra
{title2=$k[G]$}
{parent=Associative algebra}
{wiki}

The group algebra $k[G]$ is the <vector space> with basis $G$ and multiplication obtained by extending the group law $k$-bilinearly.

= Completed group algebra
{title2=$R[[G]]$}
{parent=Group algebra}

For a profinite group $G$ and a finite or profinite coefficient ring $R$, the completed group algebra is
$$
R[[G]]=\varprojlim_{U\trianglelefteq_oG}R[G/U],
$$
where $U$ ranges over the open normal subgroups. It records convergent noncommutative power series in topological generators of $G$.

= Iwasawa algebra
{c}
{parent=Completed group algebra}
{wiki}

An Iwasawa algebra is a <completed group algebra> such as $\mathbb Z_p[[G]]$ or $\mathbb F_p[[G]]$ for a compact p-adic analytic group $G$.

= Filtration on a group
{parent=Iwasawa algebra}

A filtration on a group $G$ is a function $\omega:G\to\mathbb R\cup\{\infty\}$ satisfying
$$
\omega(xy^{-1})\geq\min\{\omega(x),\omega(y)\},
\qquad
\omega([x,y])\geq\omega(x)+\omega(y).
$$
It is separated when $\omega(x)=\infty$ only for $x=1$.

= p-valuation
{c}
{parent=Filtration on a group}

For a <prime number> $p$, a p-valuation is a separated <filtration on a group> such that every $x\ne1$ satisfies
$$
\omega(x)>\frac1{p-1},
\qquad
\omega(x^p)=\omega(x)+1.
$$
A group equipped with one is called a p-valued group.

= Ordered basis of a complete p-valued group
{c}
{parent=p-valuation}

An ordered basis $(g_1,\ldots,g_d)$ of a complete finite-rank p-valued group gives each element a unique convergent expression $g_1^{\lambda_1}\cdots g_d^{\lambda_d}$ with $\lambda_i\in\mathbb Z_p$, and its valuation is the minimum of $\omega(g_i)+v_p(\lambda_i)$ over the nonzero coordinates.

= p-saturated group
{c}
{parent=p-valuation}

= p-saturated
{c}
{synonym}

A p-valued group is p-saturated when it is complete and every $x$ with
$$
\omega(x)>\frac{p}{p-1}
$$
has a pth root in the group.

= Associated graded Lie algebra of a filtered group
{title2=$\operatorname{gr}G$}
{parent=Filtration on a group}

For $G_\lambda=\{g:\omega(g)\geq\lambda\}$ and $G_{\lambda+}=\{g:\omega(g)>\lambda\}$, the associated graded group
$$
\operatorname{gr}G=\bigoplus_\lambda G_\lambda/G_{\lambda+}
$$
is a graded <Lie algebra> with bracket induced by the <group commutator>. For a p-valuation, $t\operatorname{gr}_\lambda(g)=\operatorname{gr}_{\lambda+1}(g^p)$ makes it a graded $\mathbb F_p[t]$-Lie algebra.

= Lazard filtration on a group algebra
{c}
{parent=Iwasawa algebra}

For a p-valued group $(G,\omega)$, give $p^r(g_1-1)\cdots(g_s-1)$ weight $r+\sum_i\omega(g_i)$. The spans of elements of weight at least $\lambda$ form a multiplicative filtration on $\mathbb Z_p[G]$ and its completions.

= Lazard enveloping-algebra map
{c}
{parent=Lazard filtration on a group algebra}

The initial form of $g-1$ depends only on the initial form of $g$ in $\operatorname{gr}G$. The resulting Lie map extends to a surjective graded algebra homomorphism
$$
U_{\mathbb F_p[t]}(\operatorname{gr}G)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{gr}\mathbb Z_p[G].
$$
For a complete finite-rank p-valued group, ordered-basis expansions show that it is an isomorphism.

= Center of an Iwasawa algebra of a complete p-valued group
{c}
{parent=Iwasawa algebra}

If $G$ is a complete finite-rank p-valued group with center $Z(G)$, then
$$
Z\bigl(\mathbb F_p[[G]]\bigr)=\mathbb F_p[[Z(G)]].
$$
The key facts are that the finite-conjugacy center of a p-valued group equals its center and that compatibility through the finite group-algebra quotients eliminates coefficients on infinite conjugacy classes.

= Lazard logarithm of a p-saturated group
{c}
{parent=Iwasawa algebra}

Inside the completed rational Iwasawa algebra, the convergent series
$$
\log g=\sum_{n\geq1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}n(g-1)^n
$$
sends a finite-rank p-saturated group to a $\mathbb Z_p$-Lie algebra. The <Baker--Campbell--Hausdorff formula> and its commutator expansion prove closure under addition and the <commutator> bracket.

= Primitive elements of a completed rational Iwasawa algebra
{parent=Lazard logarithm of a p-saturated group}

If $(g_1,\ldots,g_d)$ is an ordered basis of a finite-rank p-saturated group, then
$$
P(\widehat{\mathbb Q_pG})
=\bigoplus_{i=1}^d\mathbb Q_p\log(g_i),
$$
where a primitive element $x$ satisfies $\Delta(x)=x\otimes1+1\otimes x$ for the completed <Hopf algebra> coproduct.

= Left ideal
{parent=Associative algebra}

A left ideal $L$ of an <associative algebra> $A$ is a <vector subspace> satisfying $AL\subseteq L$. Equivalently, it is a submodule of the left regular $A$-module.

= Semisimple algebra
{parent=Associative algebra}
{wiki=Semisimple_algebra}

A finite-dimensional <associative algebra> is semisimple when its left regular module is a direct sum of simple modules. Equivalently, every <left ideal> is a direct summand. In particular, a semisimple algebra has no nonzero square-zero left ideal: if $L^2=0$ and a module projection $A\to L$ exists, its value $e$ at $1$ satisfies $e\in L$ and $e^2=e$, forcing $e=0$.

= Hochschild chain complex
{parent=Associative algebra}
{c}

For a $k$-algebra $R$ and an $R$-$R$-<bimodule> $M$, the Hochschild chains are
$$
C_n(R,M)=M\otimes_kR^{\otimes_kn}.
$$
Their boundary is
$$
\begin{aligned}
b(m\otimes r_1\otimes\cdots\otimes r_n)
={}&mr_1\otimes r_2\otimes\cdots\otimes r_n\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^im\otimes r_1\otimes\cdots\otimes r_ir_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes r_n\\
&+(-1)^nr_nm\otimes r_1\otimes\cdots\otimes r_{n-1}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Associativity and the bimodule axioms give $b^2=0$.

= Hochschild homology
{title2=$HH_n(R,M)$}
{c}
{parent=Hochschild chain complex}
{wiki}

The Hochschild homology of a $k$-algebra with coefficients in a bimodule is the homology
$$
HH_n(R,M)=H_n(C_\bullet(R,M),b).
$$

= Hochschild cochain complex
{parent=Associative algebra}
{c}

The Hochschild cochains are $C^n(R,M)=\operatorname{Hom}_k(R^{\otimes_kn},M)$, with coboundary
$$
\begin{aligned}
(\delta f)(r_1,\ldots,r_{n+1})
={}&r_1f(r_2,\ldots,r_{n+1})\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^n(-1)^if(r_1,\ldots,r_ir_{i+1},\ldots,r_{n+1})\\
&+(-1)^{n+1}f(r_1,\ldots,r_n)r_{n+1}.
\end{aligned}
$$

= Hochschild cohomology
{title2=$HH^n(R,M)$}
{c}
{parent=Hochschild cochain complex}
{wiki}

The Hochschild cohomology groups are
$$
HH^n(R,M)=H^n(C^\bullet(R,M),\delta).
$$

= Derivation into a bimodule
{parent=Hochschild cohomology}

A $k$-linear derivation from a $k$-algebra $R$ to an $R$-$R$-<bimodule> $M$ is a map $d:R\to M$ satisfying
$$
d(rs)=r d(s)+d(r)s.
$$

= Inner derivation
{parent=Derivation into a bimodule}
{wiki}

For $m\in M$, the formula $d_m(r)=rm-mr$ defines an inner derivation $R\to M$.

= First Hochschild cohomology as outer derivations
{title2=$HH^1(R,M)=\operatorname{Der}_k(R,M)/\operatorname{Inn}(R,M)$}
{c}
{parent=Inner derivation}

The degree-one Hochschild cocycles are exactly the maps that are a <derivation into a bimodule>, while degree-one coboundaries are exactly the <inner derivations>. Therefore
$$
HH^1(R,M)\cong\operatorname{Der}_k(R,M)/\operatorname{Inn}(R,M).
$$

= Universal bimodule derivation
{title2=$D:R\to\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}}$}
{parent=Derivation into a bimodule}

Let $\mu:R\otimes_kR\to R$ be multiplication and $\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}}=\ker\mu$, with the outer $R$-bimodule structure. Then
$$
D(r)=r\otimes1-1\otimes r
$$
is a derivation and composition with $D$ gives a natural isomorphism
$$
\operatorname{Hom}_{R-R}(\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}},M)
\cong\operatorname{Der}_k(R,M).
$$
The inverse sends $d$ to
$$
\theta_d\left(\sum_ir_i\otimes s_i\right)=\sum_id(r_i)s_i.
$$
The condition $\sum_ir_is_i=0$ makes this map left as well as right $R$-linear. Every element of $\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}}$ is a sum of terms $D(r)s$, which proves uniqueness.

= Inner derivation through the universal bimodule derivation
{parent=Universal bimodule derivation}

Under the universal isomorphism, the inner derivation $d_m(r)=rm-mr$ corresponds to
$$
\theta_m\left(\sum_ir_i\otimes s_i\right)=\sum_ir_ims_i.
$$
Thus inner derivations correspond exactly to bimodule maps on $\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}}$ that extend across the inclusion $\Omega_R^{\mathrm{nc}}\subseteq R\otimes_kR$.

= Specialization of an algebra
{parent=Associative algebra}
{wiki=Specialization_(mathematics)}

If an algebra is defined over a parameter ring $R$, a ring homomorphism $R\to k$ produces a specialized $k$-algebra by extension of scalars. Defining relations specialize by replacing every parameter by its image in $k$.

= Quiver
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki=Quiver_(mathematics)}

A quiver is a directed multigraph, allowing loops and multiple arrows.

= Sink of a quiver
{parent=Quiver}

A vertex of a <quiver> is a sink when no arrow has that vertex as its source. Dually, a source is a vertex at which no arrow ends.

= Representation of a quiver
{parent=Quiver}

A representation of a quiver over a field assigns a vector space to each vertex and a linear map to each arrow. A morphism is a compatible family of vertexwise linear maps.

= Finite representation type of a quiver
{parent=Representation of a quiver}

A <quiver> has finite representation type over a <field> when it has only finitely many <isomorphism>[isomorphism classes] of indecomposable finite-dimensional <representation of a quiver>[representations].

= Gabriel theorem
{c}
{parent=Finite representation type of a quiver}
{wiki=Gabriel's_theorem}

A connected quiver has finite representation type exactly when its underlying unoriented graph is a Dynkin diagram of type $A$, $D$, or $E$. In that case, dimension vectors give a bijection from indecomposable representations to positive roots.

= Bernstein–Gelfand–Ponomarev reflection functor
{c}
{parent=Finite representation type of a quiver}

At a sink $i$, the Bernstein–Gelfand–Ponomarev reflection functor reverses every arrow into $i$ and replaces the vector space at $i$ by the kernel of the sum of its incoming maps. Away from the simple representation supported at $i$, reflection at the resulting source is an inverse functor.

= Path algebra
{parent=Quiver}
{wiki}

The path algebra $kQ$ has the directed paths in a quiver $Q$ as a basis; multiplication concatenates composable paths and is zero otherwise. Representations of $Q$ are naturally modules over $kQ$.

= Morphism
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki=Morphism}

A morphism is a map between objects that preserves the structure under consideration. Its precise compatibility conditions depend on the category of objects.

= Endomorphism
{parent=Morphism}
{wiki}

An endomorphism is a <morphism> from an object to itself.

= Automorphism
{parent=Morphism}
{wiki}

An automorphism is an <isomorphism> from an object to itself.

= Group automorphism
{parent=Automorphism}

A group automorphism is a bijective <group homomorphism> from a group to itself.

= Characteristic subgroup
{parent=Group automorphism}
{wiki}

A subgroup is characteristic when every automorphism of the ambient group maps it to itself. A uniquely determined subgroup of a given order or structural type is characteristic.

= Isomorphism
{parent=Morphism}
{wiki}

An isomorphism is a <morphism> that has a two-sided inverse. Two objects are isomorphic when an isomorphism exists between them.

= Group isomorphism
{parent=Isomorphism}
{wiki}

A group isomorphism is a bijective <group homomorphism>. Its inverse is automatically a group homomorphism.

= Ring isomorphism
{parent=Isomorphism}

A ring isomorphism is a bijective <ring homomorphism>. It preserves all ring operations and has a ring-homomorphic inverse.

= Field
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki=Field_(mathematics)}

A field is a commutative ring in which every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse.

= Valuation
{title2=$v$}
{parent=Field}
{wiki=Valuation_(algebra)}

A valuation assigns sizes to elements of a <field> in a way compatible with multiplication and addition. In additive notation, $v(xy)=v(x)+v(y)$ and $v(x+y)\geq\min\{v(x),v(y)\}$.

= Non-Archimedean valuation
{parent=Valuation}
{wiki}

A non-Archimedean valuation satisfies the strong triangle inequality
$$
v(x+y)\geq\min\{v(x),v(y)\}.
$$
In multiplicative absolute-value notation this becomes $|x+y|\leq\max\{|x|,|y|\}$.

= Algebraically closed field
{parent=Field}
{wiki}

A field is algebraically closed when every nonconstant <polynomial> over it has a root in it, equivalently when every such polynomial splits into linear factors.

= Prime field
{parent=Field}

The prime field of a field $K$ is its smallest <subfield>. It is isomorphic to $\mathbb Q$ when $K$ has characteristic zero and to $\mathbb F_p$ when $K$ has characteristic $p>0$.

= Subfield
{parent=Field}
{wiki}

A subfield of a <field> $K$ is a subset containing the additive and multiplicative identities and closed under addition, subtraction, multiplication, and inversion of nonzero elements. It is itself a field under the operations inherited from $K$.

= Characteristic of a field
{title2=$\operatorname{char}F$}
{parent=Field}

= Characteristic
{synonym}

The characteristic of a <field> $F$ is the least positive integer $n$ for which $n\cdot1_F=0$, or zero if no such integer exists.

= Perfect field
{parent=Field}
{wiki}

A field is perfect when every algebraic extension is separable. In positive characteristic $p$, this is equivalent to surjectivity of the <Frobenius endomorphism> $x\mapsto x^p$; every <finite field> is perfect.

= Polynomial
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

A polynomial is a finite sum of monomials with coefficients in a ring.

= Polynomial function
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A polynomial function is the function obtained by evaluating a <polynomial> on a specified ring or field. Distinct polynomials can induce the same polynomial function over a finite field; for example, $x^p$ and $x$ agree on $\mathbb F_p$.

= Vector space of univariate polynomials
{title2=$\mathbb R[X]$}
{parent=Polynomial}

The real univariate polynomials form a <vector space> with the countable <Hamel basis>
$$
1,X,X^2,\ldots.
$$
The subspace of polynomials of degree at most $n$ has dimension $n+1$.

= Vieta formulas
{c}
{parent=Polynomial}

For a monic polynomial
$$
X^n+c_1X^{n-1}+\cdots+c_n=\prod_{i=1}^n(X-r_i),
$$
the Vieta formulas identify $(-1)^kc_k$ with the $k$th elementary symmetric polynomial in the roots. In particular, $\prod_i r_i=(-1)^nc_n$.

= Completing the square
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

Completing the square rewrites a quadratic polynomial as a squared linear expression plus a constant. For $a\ne0$,
$$
ax^2+bx+c
=a\left(x+\frac{b}{2a}\right)^2
+c-\frac{b^2}{4a}.
$$

= Monomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A monomial is a product of nonnegative integer powers of variables, usually multiplied by a coefficient. In variables $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ it has the form $c x_1^{a_1}\cdots x_n^{a_n}$ with $a_i\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$.

= Laurent polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A Laurent polynomial permits finitely many positive and negative integer powers, so it has the form $\sum_{k=-m}^na_kz^k$.

= Polynomial equation
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A polynomial equation has the form $p(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=0$ for a <polynomial> $p$. Its solutions are the points at which that polynomial vanishes.

= Monic polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

= Monic
{synonym}

A monic polynomial has leading coefficient one.

= Multivariate polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A multivariate polynomial is a finite linear combination of monomials in two or more variables.

= Zero of a function
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A zero of a function $f$ is an argument $a$ for which $f(a)=0$.

= Root of a polynomial
{parent=Zero of a function}

= Roots of a polynomial
{synonym}

A root of a polynomial $f$ is a scalar $a$ satisfying $f(a)=0$.

= Factor theorem
{parent=Root of a polynomial}
{wiki}

For a polynomial $f$ over a field, $f(a)=0$ exactly when $X-a$ divides $f(X)$. Polynomial division gives $f(X)=(X-a)q(X)+f(a)$.

= Multiplicity
{parent=Root of a polynomial}
{wiki=Multiplicity_(mathematics)}

Multiplicity records how many times an object occurs when repetitions carry mathematical information.

= Multiplicity of a root
{parent=Multiplicity}

The multiplicity of a root $a$ of a polynomial is the largest integer $m$ such that $(X-a)^m$ divides the polynomial.

= Multiple root
{parent=Multiplicity of a root}

A root is multiple when its <multiplicity of a root> is greater than one.

= Common root
{parent=Root of a polynomial}

A common root of polynomials $f$ and $g$ is a scalar $a$ satisfying $f(a)=g(a)=0$.

= Multilinear polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki=Multilinear_polynomial}

A multilinear polynomial has degree at most one in each variable. It has a unique expansion $f(x)=\sum_{S}a_S\prod_{i\in S}x_i$.

= Resultant
{title2=$\operatorname{Res}(f,g)$}
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

The resultant of two univariate polynomials is the determinant of their Sylvester matrix. It vanishes exactly when the polynomials have a common root over an algebraic closure, and a nonzero resultant gives Bézout identities between the two polynomials.

= Elementary symmetric polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki=Elementary_symmetric_polynomial}

The elementary symmetric polynomials in $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ are the sums of all products of $k$ distinct variables, for $k=1,\ldots,n$.

= Fundamental theorem of symmetric polynomials
{c}
{parent=Elementary symmetric polynomial}
{wiki}

Every symmetric polynomial over a commutative ring has a unique expression as a polynomial in the elementary symmetric polynomials.

= Newton identities
{parent=Elementary symmetric polynomial}
{c}
{wiki=Newton's_identities}

Newton's identities relate the elementary symmetric polynomials $e_k$ to the power sums $p_k=\sum_i x_i^k$. The first three give
$$
e_1=p_1,
\qquad
2e_2=p_1^2-p_2,
\qquad
6e_3=p_1^3-3p_1p_2+2p_3.
$$

= Irreducible polynomial
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

An irreducible polynomial is a nonconstant polynomial that cannot be written as a product of two nonconstant polynomials over its coefficient field. Over a general integral domain, neither factor may be a unit.

= Polynomial division
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki=Polynomial_long_division}

For polynomials $q$ and nonzero $Q$ over a field, there are unique polynomials $s,r$ such that $q=Qs+r$ and $\deg r<\deg Q$.

= Degree of a polynomial
{title2=$\deg$}
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki=Degree_of_a_polynomial}

= Polynomial degree
{synonym}

The degree of a nonzero polynomial is the largest exponent of a monomial having nonzero coefficient.

= Total degree of a polynomial
{parent=Degree of a polynomial}

The total degree of a multivariate <monomial> $x_1^{a_1}\cdots x_n^{a_n}$ is $a_1+\cdots+a_n$. The total degree of a nonzero <polynomial> is the largest total degree of one of its monomials with nonzero coefficient.

= Lagrange root bound over a field
{parent=Degree of a polynomial}
{c}

A nonzero <polynomial> of <degree of a polynomial>[degree] $d$ over a <field> has at most $d$ distinct roots. Indeed, each root supplies a linear factor by <polynomial division>, and induction on $d$ gives the bound.

= Polynomial length
{title2=$\mathcal L(P)$}
{parent=Polynomial}

For a polynomial $P=\sum_{\mathbf i}a_{\mathbf i}\mathbf X^{\mathbf i}$, its length is the sum of the absolute values of its coefficients:
$$
\mathcal L(P)=\sum_{\mathbf i}|a_{\mathbf i}|.
$$

= Quadratic function
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki=Quadratic_function}

A quadratic function of one variable has the form $ax^2+bx+c$ with $a\ne0$.

= Quadratic equation
{parent=Quadratic function}
{wiki}

The roots of $ax^2+bx+c=0$, where $a\ne0$, are
$$
x=\frac{-b\mathbin\pm\sqrt{b^2-4ac}}{2a}.
$$

= Discriminant
{parent=Polynomial}
{wiki}

A discriminant is a polynomial expression in the coefficients that vanishes when the associated polynomial has a repeated root.

= Quadratic discriminant
{title2=$\Delta$}
{parent=Discriminant}

The quadratic discriminant is $\Delta=b^2-4ac$ for $ax^2+bx+c$. Over the real numbers, the equation has two, one, or no real roots according as $\Delta$ is positive, zero, or negative.

= Algebraic number theory
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Algebraic norm
{title2=$N(\alpha)$}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

The algebraic norm of an element of a finite field extension is the determinant of multiplication by that element. In a quadratic subring of $\mathbb C$, it is often $N(\alpha)=\alpha\overline\alpha$.

= Multiplicativity of the algebraic norm
{parent=Algebraic norm}

For algebraic elements $\alpha$ and $\beta$,
$$
N(\alpha\beta)=N(\alpha)N(\beta).
$$
This follows because multiplication by $\alpha\beta$ is the composition of multiplication by $\beta$ and multiplication by $\alpha$, and determinants are multiplicative.

= Hermite–Minkowski theorem
{c}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

For fixed degree and bounded absolute discriminant, there are only finitely many number fields up to isomorphism. In particular, only finitely many extensions of bounded degree can be unramified outside a fixed finite set of primes when their ramification exponents are bounded.

= Number field
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

A number field is a finite <field extension> of the <rational number>[rational numbers].

= Quadratic number field
{parent=Number field}
{wiki=Quadratic_field}

A quadratic number field is a degree-two extension of $\mathbb Q$, necessarily of the form $\mathbb Q(\sqrt d)$ for a square-free integer $d\ne0,1$.

= Fundamental discriminant
{title2=$D$}
{parent=Quadratic number field}
{wiki}

A fundamental discriminant is an integer that occurs as the <field discriminant> of a <quadratic number field>. For square-free $d$, it is $d$ when $d\equiv1\pmod4$ and $4d$ otherwise.

= Algebraic conjugate
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki=Conjugate_element_(field_theory)}

The algebraic conjugates of an <algebraic number> are the roots of its <minimal polynomial>, equivalently its images under embeddings of its number field into an algebraic closure.

= Algebraic integer
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

An algebraic number is an algebraic integer when it satisfies a monic polynomial with integer coefficients. Algebraic integers are closed under addition and multiplication.

= Ring of integers of a number field
{title2=$\mathcal O_K$}
{parent=Algebraic integer}
{wiki=Ring_of_integers}

The ring of integers $\mathcal O_K$ of a <number field> $K$ is the ring of all <algebraic integers> in $K$.

= Prime ideal factorization
{parent=Ring of integers of a number field}

Every nonzero ideal of the <ring of integers of a number field> factors uniquely as a finite product of nonzero <prime ideals>. Thus each $x\in K^\times$ has integer valuations $\operatorname{ord}_{\mathfrak p}(x)$, almost all zero, determined by the factorization of its <principal ideal>.

= Ramification index of a prime ideal
{title2=$e_{\mathfrak p}$}
{parent=Prime ideal factorization}

If $\mathfrak p$ is a prime ideal of $\mathcal O_K$ above a rational prime $p$, its ramification index is the exponent $e_{\mathfrak p}$ of $\mathfrak p$ in the factorization of $(p)$. Equivalently, for the normalized ideal valuation, $e_{\mathfrak p}=v_{\mathfrak p}(p)$.

= Fundamental identity for prime decomposition
{parent=Ramification index of a prime ideal}

Let $B$ be the <integral closure> of a <Dedekind domain> $R$ in a finite separable extension $L/K$ of degree $n$. For a nonzero <prime ideal> $\mathfrak p$ of $R$, write
$$
\mathfrak pB=\prod_{\mathfrak q\mid\mathfrak p}\mathfrak q^{e_{\mathfrak q/\mathfrak p}}
$$
and let $f_{\mathfrak q/\mathfrak p}=[B/\mathfrak q:R/\mathfrak p]$. Then
$$
n=\sum_{\mathfrak q\mid\mathfrak p}e_{\mathfrak q/\mathfrak p}f_{\mathfrak q/\mathfrak p}.
$$

= Ring of integers of Q of pth root p
{title2=$\mathcal O_{\mathbb Q(\sqrt[p]{p})}=\mathbb Z[\sqrt[p]{p}]$}
{parent=Ring of integers of a number field}

For a <prime number> $p$ and $\alpha=\sqrt[p]{p}$, the polynomial $X^p-p$ is <Eisenstein> at $p$, and
$$
\mathcal O_{\mathbb Q(\alpha)}=\mathbb Z[\alpha].
$$
The <discriminant-index formula for an integral lattice> reduces the proof to the prime $p$. At the unique prime ideal above $p$, one has $v(\alpha)=1$ and $v(p)=p$, which rules out an integral element $g(\alpha)/p$ with $g\notin p\mathbb Z[X]$.

= Ring of integers of a quadratic field
{parent=Ring of integers of a number field}

For square-free $d\ne0,1$, an integral basis is $\{1,(1+\sqrt d)/2\}$ when $d\equiv1\pmod4$, and $\{1,\sqrt d\}$ otherwise. The discriminant is respectively $d$ and $4d$.

= Ring of integers of Q of square root two
{title2=$\mathcal O_{\mathbb Q(\sqrt2)}=\mathbb Z[\sqrt2]$}
{parent=Ring of integers of a quadratic field}

Since $2\not\equiv1\pmod4$, the ring of integers of $\mathbb Q(\sqrt2)$ is $\mathbb Z[\sqrt2]$, with integral basis $1,\sqrt2$ and field discriminant eight.

= Splitting of prime ideals in Galois extensions
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

In a <Galois extension>, the prime ideals above a fixed unramified prime have a common ramification index and residue degree, and the Galois group acts transitively on them.

= Splitting of rational primes in a quadratic field
{parent=Splitting of prime ideals in Galois extensions}

For a quadratic field of discriminant $D$ and a rational prime $p$, the ideal $(p)$ splits into two distinct prime ideals, remains prime, or ramifies according as the quadratic character $\chi_D(p)$ is $1$, $-1$, or $0$. The corresponding prime-ideal norms are $(p,p)$, $p^2$, and $p$, respectively.

= Ramification of a prime
{parent=Splitting of rational primes in a quadratic field}
{wiki=Ramification_(mathematics)}

A prime $p$ ramifies in a number field when its prime-ideal factorization contains a factor with exponent greater than one.

= Everywhere unramified extension of number fields
{parent=Ramification of a prime}

A finite extension of <number fields> is unramified everywhere when every finite <prime ideal> is unramified. If infinite places are included in the convention, one also requires every real place to remain real.

= Splitting of three in Q of square root minus p
{parent=Splitting of rational primes in a quadratic field}

If $p\equiv11\pmod{12}$ and $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-p})$, then $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[(1+\sqrt{-p})/2]$. The generator's minimal polynomial reduces modulo $3$ to $T(T-1)$, so $(3)$ splits into two distinct prime ideals of norm three.

= Discriminant of a biquadratic field
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

The discriminant of a biquadratic field is the product of the fundamental discriminants of its three quadratic subfields.

= Algebraic integer module criterion
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

An element $x$ is integral over $\mathbb Z$ exactly when it acts stably on some faithful nonzero finitely generated $\mathbb Z$-module containing one.

= Ring generated by algebraic integers
{parent=Algebraic integer module criterion}

A ring generated over $\mathbb Z$ by finitely many algebraic integers is a finitely generated $\mathbb Z$-module.

= Integral dependence is transitive
{parent=Algebraic integer module criterion}

If $B$ is integral over $A$ and an element is integral over $B$, then it is integral over $A$.

= Integral basis
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

An integral basis is a $\mathbb Z$-basis of the ring of integers of a number field.

= Discriminant of elements of a number field
{parent=Integral basis}

For elements $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n$ of a degree-$n$ number field with embeddings $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_n$, their discriminant is
$$
\operatorname{disc}(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n)
=\det(\sigma_i(\alpha_j))^2
=\det(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\mathbb Q}(\alpha_i\alpha_j)).
$$
It is nonzero exactly when the elements form a $\mathbb Q$-basis.

= Field discriminant
{title2=$d_K$}
{parent=Integral basis}
{wiki}

The field discriminant is the discriminant of any integral basis and controls the index of every suborder.

= Discriminant-index formula for an integral lattice
{parent=Field discriminant}

Let $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_n$ be an integral basis and let $\alpha_j=\sum_i a_{ij}\omega_i$ be a rational basis of algebraic integers, with integer matrix $A=(a_{ij})$. Their trace Gram matrices satisfy
$$
G_\alpha=A^TG_\omega A,
$$
so
$$
\Delta(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n)
=(\det A)^2d_K.
$$
The absolute discriminant is minimized exactly when $A$ is unimodular, equivalently when the $\alpha_i$ form an integral basis.

= Cubic field of discriminant minus 307
{title2=$K=\mathbb Q(\alpha),\ \alpha^3-5\alpha+8=0$}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

The polynomial $X^3-5X+8$ is irreducible over $\mathbb Q$. For
$$
\beta=\frac{\alpha+\alpha^2}{2},
$$
one has
$$
\beta^3-5\beta^2+11\beta-12=0,
\qquad
\alpha=-4+3\beta-\beta^2.
$$
The field has ring of integers $\mathbb Z[\beta]$ and field discriminant $-307$.

= Integral basis of the cubic field of discriminant minus 307
{parent=Cubic field of discriminant minus 307}

The elements
$$
1,\qquad\alpha,\qquad\beta=\frac{\alpha+\alpha^2}{2}
$$
form an integral basis. Relative to this basis, multiplication by $\beta$ has integer matrix
$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0&-4&-4\\
0&2&-1\\
1&1&3
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The basis discriminant is one quarter of the discriminant $-4\cdot307$ of $(1,\alpha,\alpha^2)$, hence is the square-free integer $-307$.

= Prime ideals above two and three in the cubic field of discriminant minus 307
{parent=Cubic field of discriminant minus 307}

For $g(X)=X^3-5X^2+11X-12$, the reductions are
$$
\overline g=X(X^2+X-1)\quad\text{over }\mathbb F_3
$$
and
$$
\overline g=X(X^2+X+1)\quad\text{over }\mathbb F_2.
$$
Both quadratic factors are irreducible, so
$$
(3)=(3,\beta)(3,\beta^2+\beta-1)
$$
and
$$
(2)=(2,\beta)(2,\beta^2+\beta+1).
$$

= Field norm
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

The field norm $N_{L/K}(x)$ is the determinant of multiplication by $x$, equivalently the product of its conjugates when the extension is separable.

= Norm form
{parent=Field norm}
{wiki}

For a finite <number field> $L/\mathbb Q$ with a rational basis $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_n$, the norm form is the homogeneous polynomial
$$
F(X_1,\ldots,X_n)
=N_{L/\mathbb Q}(X_1\omega_1+\cdots+X_n\omega_n).
$$

= Norm obstruction to being a square
{parent=Field norm}

If $x=y^2$ in a number field, then $N(x)=N(y)^2$; a norm that is not a square in the base field therefore obstructs square roots.

= Unit from norm one or minus one
{parent=Field norm}

An algebraic integer whose absolute norm is $1$ or $-1$ is a unit, because its conjugate product supplies an integral inverse.

= Minkowski embedding of a number field
{c}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki=Canonical_embedding}

For a number field of signature $(r_1,r_2)$, the Minkowski embedding sends an element to its values under the $r_1$ real embeddings and one representative from each of the $r_2$ complex-conjugate pairs. The image of the ring of integers is a full lattice in $\mathbb R^{r_1}\times\mathbb C^{r_2}$.

= Kronecker theorem on algebraic integers in the unit disk
{c}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

A nonzero algebraic integer whose conjugates all have modulus at most one is a <root of unity>. Its norm forces every conjugate to have modulus one, and bounded integral coefficient vectors show that two positive powers coincide.

= Minkowski convex body theorem
{c}
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki=Minkowski's_theorem}

If $\Lambda\subset\mathbb R^n$ is a full lattice and a measurable convex centrally symmetric set $C$ has volume greater than $2^n\operatorname{covol}(\Lambda)$, then $C$ contains a nonzero lattice point.

= Minkowski embedding of a real quadratic field
{parent=Minkowski convex body theorem}

For a real quadratic field $K$ with embeddings $\sigma_1,\sigma_2$, the image
$$
\{(\sigma_1(\alpha),\sigma_2(\alpha)):\alpha\in\mathcal O_K\}
$$
is a lattice in $\mathbb R^2$ of covolume $\sqrt{|d_K|}$, and the product of the two coordinates is the field norm.

= Imaginary quadratic Minkowski disk bound
{parent=Minkowski convex body theorem}

Let $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-d})$ for square-free $d>0$ with $d\not\equiv3\pmod4$, so $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[\sqrt{-d}]$. An integral ideal $I$ is a lattice in $\mathbb C\simeq\mathbb R^2$ of covolume $\sqrt d\,N(I)$. Applying the <Minkowski convex body theorem> to a disk gives a nonzero $\alpha\in I$ satisfying
$$
0<|N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\alpha)|
\le\frac{4\sqrt d}{\pi}N(I).
$$

= Mahler measure
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

If $f(X)=a_d\prod_{j=1}^d(X-\alpha_j)$, its Mahler measure is
$$
M(f)=|a_d|\prod_{j=1}^d\max\{1,|\alpha_j|\}.
$$

= Mahler-measure norm argument at measure two
{parent=Mahler measure}

Let an irreducible $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$ have $M(f)=2$ and a root outside the closed unit disk. Its leading coefficient must have absolute value one. The product $B$ of all roots outside the unit disk is then an algebraic integer with $|B|=2$; conjugate pairs make $B$ real, so $B=\pm2$. The quotient of the norm of any root by $B$ is both rational and an algebraic integer, and its absolute value is at most one. It is therefore $\pm1$, so every root has absolute norm two.

= Mahler measure bounded by polynomial length
{parent=Mahler measure}

For every complex polynomial $P$,
$$
M(P)\leq\mathcal L(P).
$$
This follows from Jensen's formula, or directly by bounding $|P(z)|$ by its <polynomial length> on the unit circle and using the integral formula for the <Mahler measure>.

= Place of a number field
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki=Place_(mathematics)}

A place of a <number field> $K$ is an equivalence class of <absolute values on a field>. Its Archimedean places come from real embeddings and conjugate pairs of complex embeddings. Its non-Archimedean places correspond to nonzero prime ideals of the <ring of integers of a number field>.

= Archimedean place
{parent=Place of a number field}
{wiki}

A real embedding $K\hookrightarrow\mathbb R$ gives a real Archimedean place of local degree $1$. A conjugate pair of complex embeddings $K\hookrightarrow\mathbb C$ gives a complex Archimedean place of local degree $2$.

= Non-Archimedean place of a number field
{parent=Place of a number field}

If a prime ideal $\mathfrak p$ of $K$ lies over the <prime number> $p$ with <ramification index of a prime ideal> $e_{\mathfrak p}$, its normalized absolute value is
$$
|x|_{\mathfrak p}=p^{-\operatorname{ord}_{\mathfrak p}(x)/e_{\mathfrak p}}.
$$
It extends the usual <p-adic absolute value> on $\mathbb Q$. Its local degree is $[K_{\mathfrak p}:\mathbb Q_p]=e_{\mathfrak p}f_{\mathfrak p}$, where $f_{\mathfrak p}$ is the <residue-field degree>.

= Local degree of a place
{parent=Place of a number field}

For a place $v$ of a <number field> $K$, the local degree is $d_v=[K_v:\mathbb Q_v]$. Thus $d_v=1$ at a real place, $d_v=2$ at a complex place, and $d_v=e_vf_v$ at a finite place over $p$. For each rational place, the local degrees above it sum to $[K:\mathbb Q]$.

= Height function
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

A height function measures the arithmetic complexity of an algebraic number or a point of an arithmetic variety.

= Absolute multiplicative Weil height
{c}
{title2=$H(\alpha)$}
{parent=Height function}

For an algebraic number $\alpha$ in a number field $L$, the absolute multiplicative Weil height is
$$
H(\alpha)=\prod_{v\in M_L}\max(1,|\alpha|_v)^{[L_v:\mathbb Q_v]/[L:\mathbb Q]}.
$$
It is independent of the field $L$ containing $\alpha$, satisfies $H(\alpha^n)=H(\alpha)^{|n|}$, and obeys $H(\alpha+\beta)\leq2H(\alpha)H(\beta)$.

= Height of a rational number
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

For coprime integers $p,q$ with $q>0$,
$$
H(p/q)=\max\{|p|,q\}.
$$
This follows directly from the real and <p-adic absolute value>[p-adic absolute values], or from the <height-Mahler measure formula> for the primitive polynomial $qX-p$.

= Absolute logarithmic Weil height
{c}
{title2=$h(\alpha)$}
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

The absolute logarithmic Weil height is $h(\alpha)=\log H(\alpha)$.

= Projective height
{title2=$H_p$}
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

For a nonzero vector $\mathbf a=(a_0,\ldots,a_n)$ over a <number field> $K$, its absolute projective height is
$$
H_p(\mathbf a)=\prod_{v\in M_K}\max_i|a_i|_v^{d_v/[K:\mathbb Q]}.
$$
The <product formula> makes this unchanged by multiplying all coordinates by the same nonzero scalar. The projective height of a <linear form> is the projective height of its coefficient vector.

= Naive polynomial height
{title2=$H(P)$}
{parent=Projective height}

The naive height of a polynomial is the largest absolute value of its coefficients. For a primitive polynomial in $\mathbb Z[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$, this equals the <projective height> of its coefficient vector.

= Height-Mahler measure formula
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

If the primitive minimal polynomial of an algebraic number $\alpha$ has degree $d$, then
$$
H(\alpha)^d=M(f_\alpha).
$$

= Height bound for a polynomial evaluation
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

Let $P,Q\in\mathbb Z[X_1,\ldots,X_k]$ have degree at most $n_j$ in $X_j$. Then
$$
H\left(\frac{P(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_k)}
{Q(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_k)}\right)
\leq
\max\{\mathcal L(P),\mathcal L(Q)\}
\prod_{j=1}^kH(\alpha_j)^{n_j}
$$
whenever the quotient is defined. At each non-Archimedean place, the integral coefficients contribute at most one; at the Archimedean places, the <triangle inequality> contributes the <polynomial length>. Multiplying these local estimates and using the <product formula> gives the claim.

= Liouville height inequality
{c}
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}

If $\gamma\ne0$ is algebraic of degree at most $D$, then every embedding satisfies
$$
|\gamma|\geq H(\gamma)^{-D}.
$$
Indeed, $H(\gamma^{-1})=H(\gamma)$ and the contribution of one Archimedean place to the height bounds $|\gamma|^{-1/D}$.

= Northcott theorem
{c}
{parent=Absolute multiplicative Weil height}
{wiki=Northcott's_theorem}

There are only finitely many algebraic numbers of bounded <field extension>[degree] and bounded <absolute multiplicative Weil height>. The finite set can be enumerated effectively from the bounded coefficients of their primitive minimal polynomials.

= Product formula
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

For a nonzero element $x$ of a <number field> $L$, normalized absolute values satisfy
$$
\prod_{v\in M_L}|x|_v^{[L_v:\mathbb Q_v]}=1.
$$
For an algebraic integer $x$, factor the principal ideal $(x)$ into prime ideals. Its ideal norm is both the product of the finite-place contributions with inverse exponent and the absolute value of the product of its Archimedean conjugates. Equating the two expressions proves the formula for algebraic integers, and writing an arbitrary $x\in L^\times$ as a quotient proves the general case.

= Dirichlet unit theorem
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{c}
{wiki=Dirichlet's_unit_theorem}

If a number field has $r$ real embeddings and $s$ conjugate pairs of complex embeddings, then
$$
\mathcal O_K^\times\cong\mu(K)\times\mathbb Z^{r+s-1},
$$
where $\mu(K)$ is the finite group of roots of unity in $K$.

= Units of the quadratic field Q square root of five
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

For $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt5)$,
$$
\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z\left[\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}\right].
$$
Writing $\phi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, the full unit group is
$$
\mathcal O_K^\times=\{\mathord\pm\phi^n:n\in\mathbb Z\}.
$$

= Units of Q of square root two
{title2=$\mathcal O_{\mathbb Q(\sqrt2)}^\times$}
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

The unit $\varepsilon=1+\sqrt2$ has norm minus one and is the least unit greater than one in the positive real embedding. <Dirichlet unit theorem> therefore gives
$$
\mathbb Z[\sqrt2]^\times
=\{\mathord\pm(1+\sqrt2)^n:n\in\mathbb Z\}.
$$

= Units congruent to one modulo 3 plus square root two
{title2=$\{u\in\mathbb Z[\sqrt2]^\times:u\equiv1\pmod{3+\sqrt2}\}$}
{parent=Units of Q of square root two}

Reduction modulo $(3+\sqrt2)$ identifies $\mathbb Z[\sqrt2]/(3+\sqrt2)$ with $\mathbb F_7$, sends $\sqrt2$ to $4$, and sends $\varepsilon=1+\sqrt2$ to $5$. Since $5$ has order six and $5^3=-1$ in $\mathbb F_7^\times$, the kernel on units is the infinite cyclic group
$$
\left\langle-(1+\sqrt2)^3\right\rangle
=\langle-7-5\sqrt2\rangle.
$$

= Finite relative unit quotient over a real quadratic field
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

If $K$ is real quadratic and $L/K$ is a number-field extension with $\mathcal O_L^\times/\mathcal O_K^\times$ finite, then the unit ranks agree. If $d=[L:K]$ and $L$ has signature $(r_1,r_2)$, this forces
$$
r_1+r_2=2,\qquad r_1+2r_2=2d.
$$
For a proper extension the only possibility is $d=2$ and signature $(0,2)$. For $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt5)$, the example $L=K(i)$ has this signature and a nontrivial finite quotient because $i$ is a new root of unity.

= Logarithmic embedding of number field units
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

Choose the real embeddings $\sigma_i$ and one embedding $\tau_j$ from each complex-conjugate pair. The weighted logarithmic embedding is
$$
\operatorname{Log}(u)=
\bigl(\log|\sigma_1u|,\ldots,\log|\sigma_ru|,
2\log|\tau_1u|,\ldots,2\log|\tau_su|\bigr).
$$
For a unit, the sum of these coordinates is $\log|N_{K/\mathbb Q}(u)|=0$.

= Kernel of the logarithmic unit embedding
{parent=Logarithmic embedding of number field units}

The kernel of $\operatorname{Log}:\mathcal O_K^\times\to\mathbb R^{r+s}$ is exactly $\mu(K)$. If every conjugate of a unit has modulus one, all its powers lie in a bounded subset of the discrete Minkowski lattice $\mathcal O_K$, so two powers coincide and the unit is a root of unity.

= Dirichlet unit lattice
{parent=Logarithmic embedding of number field units}
{c}

The logarithms of a fundamental system of $r+s-1$ units form a basis of the hyperplane
$$
H=\{x\in\mathbb R^{r+s}:x_1+\cdots+x_{r+s}=0\},
$$
and their integer span is a full lattice in $H$.

= Archimedean balancing of a principal ideal generator
{parent=Dirichlet unit lattice}

Every principal ideal $I$ of a number field has a generator $\alpha$ satisfying
$$
|\sigma(\alpha)|\leq C_KN(I)^{1/[K:\mathbb Q]}
$$
at every archimedean embedding. Multiply an arbitrary generator by a unit so that its logarithmic unit-lattice coordinates lie in a fixed bounded fundamental parallelepiped.

= Units of Q of square root ten
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

For $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{10})$,
$$
\mathcal O_K^\times
=\{\mathord\pm(3+\sqrt{10})^n:n\in\mathbb Z\}.
$$
The fundamental unit $3+\sqrt{10}$ has norm minus one.

= Units of Q of square root minus three
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

For $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$ and $\omega=(-1+\sqrt{-3})/2$,
$$
\mathcal O_K^\times
=\{\mathord\pm1,\mathord\pm\omega,\mathord\pm\omega^2\}
\cong C_6.
$$

= Unit group of the fifth cyclotomic field
{parent=Dirichlet unit theorem}

For a primitive fifth root of unity $\zeta$,
$$
\mathcal O_{\mathbb Q(\zeta)}^\times
=\left\{\mathord\pm\zeta^a
\left(\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}\right)^b:a,b\in\mathbb Z\right\}
\cong C_{10}\times\mathbb Z.
$$

= Units of the fifth cyclotomic field are real up to roots of unity
{parent=Unit group of the fifth cyclotomic field}

Let $L=\mathbb Q(\zeta_5)$ and let complex conjugation be denoted by a bar. For $u\in\mathcal O_L^\times$, every conjugate of $u/\bar u$ has modulus one, so the <kernel of the logarithmic unit embedding> shows that $u/\bar u$ is a root of unity. Reduction modulo $1-\zeta_5$ gives $u/\bar u\equiv1$, so this root belongs to $\mu_5$. Squaring permutes $\mu_5$, hence multiplication by a suitable root of unity makes $u$ real. The real unit belongs to $\mathbb Q(\sqrt5)$ and the <units of the quadratic field Q square root of five> finish the calculation.

= Class field theory
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

= Global class field theory
{synonym}

Global class field theory classifies finite abelian extensions of a number field using ideal-class or idele-class quotients and the Artin reciprocity map.

= Modulus of a number field
{parent=Class field theory}

A modulus is a formal product $\mathfrak m=\mathfrak m_0\mathfrak m_\infty$, where $\mathfrak m_0$ is a nonzero integral ideal and $\mathfrak m_\infty$ is a product of distinct real places.

= Ray class group
{title2=$\operatorname{Cl}_{\mathfrak m}(K)$}
{parent=Modulus of a number field}
{wiki}

The ray class group modulo $\mathfrak m$ is $I_K(\mathfrak m)/P_K(\mathfrak m)$, where the numerator consists of fractional ideals prime to $\mathfrak m_0$ and the denominator consists of $(\alpha)$ with $\alpha\equiv1$ at the finite modulus and positive at every real place in $\mathfrak m_\infty$.

= Ray class exact sequence
{parent=Ray class group}

For a finite modulus $\mathfrak m$, there is an exact sequence
$$
\mathcal O_K^\times\longrightarrow
(\mathcal O_K/\mathfrak m)^\times\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Cl}_{\mathfrak m}(K)\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Cl}(K)\longrightarrow1.
$$
With real places in the modulus, the residue-unit term and the unit map also record the required signs.

= Ray class number formula
{parent=Ray class group}

If $U_{\mathfrak m,1}$ consists of units congruent to one modulo $\mathfrak m_0$ and positive at the selected real places, then
$$
|\operatorname{Cl}_{\mathfrak m}(K)|
=h_K\frac{2^{|\mathfrak m_\infty|}N\mathfrak m_0\prod_{\mathfrak p\mid\mathfrak m_0}(1-N\mathfrak p^{-1})}
{[\mathcal O_K^\times:U_{\mathfrak m,1}]}.
$$

= Artin symbol
{title2=$\left(\frac{L/K}{\mathfrak p}\right)$}
{parent=Class field theory}
{c}
{wiki}

For an unramified prime $\mathfrak p$ in an abelian extension $L/K$, the Artin symbol is the unique Galois element acting on residue fields by $x\mapsto x^{N\mathfrak p}$.

= Ideal-theoretic decomposition law
{parent=Artin symbol}

For an unramified prime $\mathfrak p$ in a finite abelian extension $L/K$, the residue degree equals the order of its <Artin symbol>. Consequently the number of primes above $\mathfrak p$ is $[L:K]$ divided by that order.

= Artin reciprocity law
{parent=Artin symbol}
{c}
{wiki}

= Artin reciprocity map
{synonym}
{c}

The Artin map sends an ideal prime to the ramified primes to the product of its Frobenius elements. For a suitable modulus, its kernel is the norm or congruence subgroup associated with the abelian extension.

= Conductor of an abelian extension
{parent=Class field theory}

The conductor $\mathfrak f_{L/K}$ is the smallest modulus through which the <Artin reciprocity map> for a finite abelian extension $L/K$ factors. Its finite prime divisors are exactly the ramified primes, with exponents measuring the depth of ramification.

= Existence theorem of global class field theory
{parent=Class field theory}

Finite abelian extensions of $K$ correspond contravariantly to congruence subgroups $H$ with $P_K(\mathfrak m)\subseteq H\subseteq I_K(\mathfrak m)$, and the Artin map identifies $I_K(\mathfrak m)/H$ with the Galois group.

= Ray class field
{parent=Existence theorem of global class field theory}
{wiki}

The ray class field modulo $\mathfrak m$ is the maximal abelian extension whose Artin map factors through the ray class group; its degree is the ray class number.

= Adele ring
{title2=$\mathbb A_K$}
{parent=Class field theory}
{wiki=Adèle_ring}

The adele ring is the restricted product of the completions $K_v$ with respect to the valuation rings $\mathcal O_v$ at finite places.

= Restricted product
{title2=$\prod_i'(X_i,U_i)$}
{parent=Adele ring}
{wiki}

Given spaces $X_i$ with distinguished open subsets $U_i$, their restricted product consists of tuples $(x_i)$ for which $x_i\in U_i$ at all but finitely many indices.

= Restricted product topology
{parent=Restricted product}

A basic open set in a restricted product $\prod_v'(X_v,U_v)$ is a product of open local sets that equals the distinguished subset $U_v$ at all but finitely many indices.

= Idele group
{title2=$\mathbb I_K$}
{parent=Adele ring}

The idele group is the restricted product $\prod_v'K_v^\times$ with respect to $\mathcal O_v^\times$ at finite places.

= Restricted product topology on the idele group
{parent=Idele group}

The idele topology is the restricted product topology with respect to $\mathcal O_v^\times$. It is also the subspace topology induced by $x\mapsto(x,x^{-1})$ from $\mathbb A_K\times\mathbb A_K$.

= Idèle class group
{title2=$C_K$}
{parent=Idele group}
{wiki}

The idele class group is $C_K=\mathbb I_K/K^\times$, where $K^\times$ is embedded diagonally.

= Idelic congruence subgroup
{title2=$C_K(\mathfrak m)$}
{parent=Idèle class group}

The ideles congruent to one modulo $\mathfrak m$ have finite components in $1+\mathfrak p^{n_\mathfrak p}$ at primes in the finite modulus and positive components at selected real places. Their image in $C_K$ is the idelic congruence subgroup.

= Norm group of an abelian extension
{title2=$N_{L/K}C_L$}
{parent=Idèle class group}

For a finite abelian extension $L/K$, the norm group is the image of the idele-class norm $C_L\to C_K$. Class field theory gives $C_K/N_{L/K}C_L\cong\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)$.

= Idele norm
{title2=$N_{L/K}:\mathbb I_L\to\mathbb I_K$}
{parent=Norm group of an abelian extension}

The $v$-component of the idele norm is $\prod_{w\mid v}N_{L_w/K_v}(x_w)$. It descends to an <idèle class group> norm $C_L\to C_K$.

= Nonabelian norm-residue kernel theorem
{parent=Norm group of an abelian extension}

For a finite Galois extension $L/K$ with group $G$, the kernel of the global reciprocity map $C_K\to G^{\mathrm{ab}}$ is $N_{L/K}C_L$. It follows that this norm group equals the norm group from the maximal abelian subextension $L^{[G,G]}/K$.

= Kronecker–Weber theorem
{c}
{parent=Class field theory}
{wiki}

Every finite abelian extension of $\mathbb Q$ is contained in a cyclotomic field.

= Hilbert class field
{c}
{parent=Class field theory}
{wiki}

The Hilbert class field is the maximal unramified abelian extension of a number field. Its Galois group is canonically the ideal class group.

= Hilbert class field of Q of square root minus six
{title2=$H_{\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-6})}=\mathbb Q(\sqrt2,\sqrt{-3})$}
{c}
{parent=Hilbert class field}

The <ideal class group> of $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-6})$ has order two, and
$$
H_K=K(\sqrt2)=\mathbb Q(\sqrt2,\sqrt{-3}).
$$
This quadratic extension of $K$ is unramified at every finite prime and therefore has the degree required of the <Hilbert class field>.

= Dirichlet character
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

A Dirichlet character modulo $m$ is a homomorphism $(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times\to\mathbb C^\times$, extended periodically to integers by zero on numbers not coprime to $m$.

= Orthogonality of Dirichlet characters
{c}
{parent=Dirichlet character}

For reduced residue classes $a,n$ modulo $q$,
$$
\frac1{\varphi(q)}
\sum_{\chi\bmod q}\overline{\chi(a)}\chi(n)
=
\begin{cases}
1,&n\equiv a\pmod q,\\
0,&n\not\equiv a\pmod q.
\end{cases}
$$
This is the orthogonality relation for the characters of the finite <abelian group> $(\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z)^\times$.

= Kronecker symbol
{title2=$\left(\frac an\right)$}
{parent=Dirichlet character}
{wiki}

The Kronecker symbol extends the <Jacobi symbol> to all integer denominators. For a fundamental discriminant $D$, the function $\chi_D(n)=\left(\frac Dn\right)$ is the primitive quadratic <Dirichlet character> associated with $\mathbb Q(\sqrt D)$.

= Dirichlet L-function
{title2=$L(s,\chi)$}
{parent=Dirichlet character}
{c}
{wiki}

For $\operatorname{Re}s>1$,
$$
L(s,\chi)=\sum_{n\geq1}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^s}
=\prod_p(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-1}.
$$

= Dirichlet density
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{c}
{wiki}

A set $S$ of prime ideals has Dirichlet density
$$
\delta(S)=\lim_{s\to1^+}
\frac{\sum_{\mathfrak p\in S}N\mathfrak p^{-s}}
{\log(1/(s-1))},
$$
when the limit exists.

= Chebotarev density theorem
{c}
{parent=Dirichlet density}
{wiki}

For a finite Galois extension $L/K$ and a conjugacy class $C$ in its Galois group, the unramified primes with Frobenius class $C$ have density $|C|/|\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)|$.

= Field trace
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

The field trace $\operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(x)$ is the trace of multiplication by $x$, equivalently the sum of its conjugates in a separable extension.

= Trace pairing
{parent=Field trace}

For a finite field extension $L/K$, the trace pairing is the <bilinear form>
$$
(x,y)\longmapsto\operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(xy).
$$
It is nondegenerate exactly when $L/K$ is separable.

= Trace-dual lattice
{parent=Trace pairing}

For an $R$-lattice $M$ in $L$, its trace-dual lattice is
$$
M^\vee=\{x\in L:\operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(xM)\subseteq R\}.
$$
For the integral closure $S$ of $R$, the trace dual $S^\vee$ is the <inverse different>.

= Discriminant ideal
{title2=$\operatorname{disc}(S/R)$}
{parent=Trace pairing}

For the integral closure $S$ of a <Dedekind domain> $R$ in a finite separable extension, the discriminant ideal is locally generated by
$$
\det\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(\alpha_i\alpha_j)\bigr),
$$
where $(\alpha_i)$ is a local $R$-basis of $S$. Intrinsically it is the image of $(\det_R S)^{\otimes2}$ under the determinant of the <trace pairing>.

= Relative discriminant
{title2=$\mathfrak d_{L/K}$}
{parent=Discriminant ideal}

The relative discriminant $\mathfrak d_{L/K}$ of a finite extension of <number fields> is the <discriminant ideal> of $\mathcal O_L$ over $\mathcal O_K$. It satisfies
$$
|d_L|=|d_K|^{[L:K]}N_{K/\mathbb Q}(\mathfrak d_{L/K}),
$$
and its prime divisors are exactly the ramified finite primes.

= Trace form of a number field
{title2=$B(x,y)=\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\mathbb Q}(xy)$}
{parent=Field trace}
{wiki=Trace_form}

The trace form of a number field $K$ is the symmetric bilinear form
$$
B(x,y)=\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\mathbb Q}(xy).
$$

= Nondegeneracy of the trace form of a number field
{parent=Trace form of a number field}

For nonzero $x\in K$, choose $y=x^{-1}$. Then
$$
B(x,y)=\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\mathbb Q}(1)=[K:\mathbb Q]\ne0,
$$
so the trace form is <nondegenerate bilinear form>[nondegenerate].

= Trace and norm integrality criterion for a quadratic extension
{parent=Field trace}

In a quadratic extension $L/K$, an element is integral exactly when its relative trace and norm lie in $\mathcal O_K$.

= Fractional ideal
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

A fractional ideal of a number field is a nonzero $\mathcal O_K$-submodule $I\subset K$ for which $dI\subseteq\mathcal O_K$ for some nonzero $d\in\mathcal O_K$.

= Integral ideal
{parent=Fractional ideal}

An integral ideal is a fractional ideal contained in the ring of integers $\mathcal O_K$.

= Ideal norm
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

For a nonzero integral ideal $I\subseteq\mathcal O_K$, its norm is the finite index $N(I)=|\mathcal O_K/I|$. Norms multiply under ideal multiplication.

= Norm of a fractional ideal
{title2=$N_{L/K}(I)$}
{parent=Ideal norm}

For a fractional ideal $I$ of $\mathcal O_L$, the relative ideal norm is the fractional ideal of $\mathcal O_K$ obtained multiplicatively from prime-ideal norms. Equivalently, its local valuation is the determinant valuation of $I$ as an $\mathcal O_K$-lattice relative to $\mathcal O_L$.

= Ideal class group
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{wiki}

The ideal class group is the group of nonzero fractional ideals modulo principal fractional ideals.

= Ideal class
{parent=Ideal class group}

An ideal class is an element of the <ideal class group>, represented by a nonzero <fractional ideal>. Two fractional ideals represent the same class exactly when their quotient is a <principal ideal>.

= Class number
{title2=$h_K$}
{parent=Ideal class group}
{wiki}

The class number of a number field is the order $h_K=|\operatorname{Cl}(K)|$ of its <ideal class group>.

= Minkowski bound for ideal classes
{parent=Ideal class group}
{c}
{wiki=Minkowski's_bound}

If a number field of degree $n$ and signature $(r_1,r_2)$ has discriminant $d_K$, every ideal class contains an integral ideal $I$ with
$$
N(I)\leq
\left(\frac4\pi\right)^{r_2}
\frac{n!}{n^n}\sqrt{|d_K|}.
$$

= Minkowski lower bound for a number-field discriminant
{c}
{parent=Minkowski bound for ideal classes}

Since every nonzero integral ideal has norm at least one, the <Minkowski bound for ideal classes> implies
$$
|d_K|\geq
\left(\frac\pi4\right)^{2r_2}
\left(\frac{n^n}{n!}\right)^2
$$
for every number field of degree $n$ and signature $(r_1,r_2)$.

= Class group of Q of cube root of three
{title2=$\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb Q(\sqrt[3]{3}))$}
{parent=Minkowski bound for ideal classes}
{c}

For $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt[3]{3})$ with $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[\sqrt[3]{3}]$, the field discriminant is $-243$ and the Minkowski bound is $8\sqrt3/\pi<5$. The prime ideals above $2$ and $3$ are principal, so every ideal class has a principal representative and
$$
\operatorname{Cl}(K)=1.
$$

= Ideal class group of Q of square root of minus one hundred sixty-three
{title2=$\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-163}))$}
{parent=Minkowski bound for ideal classes}
{c}

The imaginary quadratic field $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-163})$ has discriminant $-163$, and its <Minkowski bound for ideal classes> is
$$
\frac2\pi\sqrt{163}<9.
$$
The polynomial $X^2-X+41$ defining its <ring of integers of a quadratic field> has no root modulo $2,3,5,$ or $7$, so these rational primes are inert. Factoring an integral ideal of norm at most eight into <prime ideals> then shows that its only possible nontrivial factor is the principal ideal $(2)$, of norm four. Every ideal class is therefore principal, and
$$
\operatorname{Cl}(K)=1.
$$

= Ideal class group of Q of square root of minus thirty-three
{title2=$\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-33}))$}
{parent=Minkowski bound for ideal classes}
{c}

The field has ring of integers $\mathbb Z[\sqrt{-33}]$, discriminant $-132$, and Minkowski bound below eight. The nonprincipal ramified ideals above two and three generate four classes, and the split primes above seven introduce no new class. Hence
$$
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-33}))
\cong C_2\times C_2.
$$

= Ideal class group of Q of square root of minus twenty-two
{title2=$\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-22}))\cong C_2$}
{parent=Minkowski bound for ideal classes}
{c}

The ring of integers is $\mathbb Z[\sqrt{-22}]$, its discriminant is $-88$, and its <Minkowski bound for ideal classes> is $4\sqrt{22}/\pi<6$. The primes three and five are inert, while
$$
(2)=\mathfrak p_2^2,
\qquad
\mathfrak p_2=(2,\sqrt{-22}).
$$
The ideal $\mathfrak p_2$ is nonprincipal because $a^2+22b^2=2$ has no integer solution. Every class is represented by an ideal of norm at most five, so $[\mathfrak p_2]$ is the only possible nontrivial class and the class group is $C_2$.

= No integral solutions to y cubed equals x squared plus twenty-two
{title2=$y^3=x^2+22$}
{parent=Ideal class group of Q of square root of minus twenty-two}

The equation $y^3=x^2+22$ has no integer solutions. Factoring it in $\mathbb Z[\sqrt{-22}]$, the two conjugate factors are coprime. <Unique factorization of ideals in a number field> and the absence of three-torsion in the class group force $x+\sqrt{-22}$ to be a cube up to a unit. Writing it as $(a+b\sqrt{-22})^3$ makes its $\sqrt{-22}$ coefficient
$$
b(3a^2-22b^2)=1,
$$
which has no integer solution.

= Euler prime-generating quadratic from class number one
{parent=Ideal class group}
{c}

Let $m\geq2$, let $p=4m-1$ be prime, and suppose $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-p})$ has class number one. If $n\geq0$ and $n^2+n+m<m^2$, then $n^2+n+m$ is prime. The key identity is the <algebraic norm>
$$
n^2+n+m=N\left(n+\frac{1+\sqrt{-p}}2\right).
$$
A hypothetical prime divisor below $m$ would split and yield an algebraic integer of that norm, but the positive norm form $(a^2+pb^2)/4$ represents no prime below $m$.

= Order of an ideal class
{parent=Ideal class group}

The order of the class of a fractional ideal $I$ is the least positive integer $n$ for which $I^n$ is principal.

= Odd-order obstruction for a split prime in an imaginary quadratic field
{parent=Order of an ideal class}

Let $p\equiv11\pmod{12}$, $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-p})$, and let $\mathfrak p$ be a prime-ideal factor of $(3)$. If $p>3^{n+2}$ and $n$ is odd, then $[\mathfrak p]$ does not have order $n$. Otherwise $\mathfrak p^n=(\alpha)$ would give
$$
4\cdot3^n=a^2+pb^2
$$
for $\alpha=(a+b\sqrt{-p})/2$. The bound forces $b=0$, after which $3^n=N(\alpha)$ is an integer square, impossible for odd $n$.

= Bounded-norm ideal representatives
{parent=Ideal class group}

If every integral ideal $I$ contains nonzero $\alpha$ with $|N(\alpha)|\leq C N(I)$, then $(\alpha)I^{-1}$ is an integral representative of $[I]^{-1}$ with norm at most $C$.

= Finiteness of the ideal class group
{parent=Ideal class group}

Bounded-norm representatives prove class-group finiteness because only finitely many integral ideals have norm at most a fixed constant.

= Ideal classes are torsion
{parent=Ideal class group}

Every element of a finite ideal class group has finite order. Therefore every nonzero ideal has a positive power that is principal.

= Unique factorization of ideals in a number field
{parent=Algebraic number theory}

Every nonzero proper ideal of a number ring factors uniquely as a finite product of positive powers of distinct nonzero prime ideals.

= Dedekind factorization theorem
{parent=Unique factorization of ideals in a number field}
{c}
{wiki=Dedekind's_theorem}

If $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[\theta]$ and the minimal polynomial of $\theta$ factors modulo a rational prime $p$ as
$$
\overline f=\prod_i\overline g_i^{e_i},
$$
then
$$
(p)=\prod_i(p,g_i(\theta))^{e_i},
$$
and the prime ideal $(p,g_i(\theta))$ has norm $p^{\deg g_i}$.

= Monogenic number field
{parent=Dedekind factorization theorem}
{wiki=Monogenic_field}

A number field $K$ is monogenic when its ring of integers has a power integral basis, meaning $\mathcal O_K=\mathbb Z[\theta]$ for some algebraic integer $\theta$.

= Completely split small-prime obstruction to monogenicity
{parent=Monogenic number field}

Let $K$ have degree $n$, and suppose a rational prime $p$ factors into $n$ distinct prime ideals of norm $p$. If $n>p$, then $K$ is not monogenic. Otherwise the <Dedekind factorization theorem> would force the degree-$n$ minimal polynomial of a power-basis generator to have $n$ distinct linear factors over $\mathbb F_p$, although only the $p$ polynomials $X-a$ are available.

= Dedekind zeta function
{parent=Algebraic number theory}
{c}
{wiki}

For a number field $K$, the Dedekind zeta function is formally
$$
\zeta_K(s)=\sum_{0\ne\mathfrak a\subseteq\mathcal O_K}N(\mathfrak a)^{-s}.
$$

= Euler product for the Dedekind zeta function
{parent=Dedekind zeta function}

Unique factorization of nonzero ideals and multiplicativity of the ideal norm give the formal identity
$$
\zeta_K(s)=\prod_{\mathfrak p}
\left(1-N(\mathfrak p)^{-s}\right)^{-1},
$$
where the product ranges over nonzero prime ideals.

= Quadratic Dedekind zeta factorization
{parent=Dedekind zeta function}

For a quadratic field $K$ of discriminant $D$,
$$
\zeta_K(s)=\zeta_{\mathbb Q}(s)L(\chi_D,s),
$$
where $\chi_D(p)$ is $1$, $-1$, or $0$ according as $(p)$ splits, remains prime, or ramifies in $\mathcal O_K$. Comparing the three possible local Euler factors proves the formal identity.

= Analytic class number formula
{parent=Dedekind zeta function}
{wiki}

For a number field of signature $(r_1,r_2)$, roots-of-unity count $w_K$, regulator $R_K$, class number $h_K$, and discriminant $d_K$,
$$
\operatorname*{Res}_{s=1}\zeta_K(s)
=\frac{2^{r_1}(2\pi)^{r_2}h_KR_K}{w_K\sqrt{|d_K|}}.
$$

= Quadratic class number formula
{parent=Analytic class number formula}

For an imaginary quadratic field of fundamental discriminant $D<-4$,
$$
h(D)=\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{\pi}L(1,\chi_D).
$$
Equivalent finite character-sum forms follow by evaluating the odd quadratic <Dirichlet L-function> at one.

= Galois theory
{c}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Fundamental theorem of Galois theory
{c}
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

For a finite <Galois extension> $L/K$, intermediate fields correspond inclusion-reversingly to subgroups of $\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)$. Normal subgroups correspond to Galois intermediate extensions.

= Kummer theory
{c}
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

When a field $K$ contains the $n$th roots of unity and its characteristic does not divide $n$, Kummer theory identifies cyclic extensions of degree dividing $n$ with suitable classes in $K^\times/(K^\times)^n$. Cohomologically,
$$
H^1(G_K,\mu_n)\simeq K^\times/(K^\times)^n.
$$

= Kummer extension
{c}
{parent=Kummer theory}

When a field $K$ contains the relevant roots of unity, adjoining an $n$th root of an element of $K$ gives a Kummer extension. Such extensions describe abelian extensions of exponent dividing $n$.

= Galois cohomology
{title2=$H^i(G,M)$}
{c}
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

Galois cohomology is the <group cohomology> of a Galois group acting on a module. For a discrete $G$-module $M$, the first group is the quotient of crossed homomorphisms $c(\sigma\tau)=c(\sigma)+\sigma c(\tau)$ by maps $c(\sigma)=\sigma m-m$.

= Frobenius endomorphism
{title2=$x\mapsto x^p$}
{parent=Galois theory}
{c}
{wiki}

In a field of characteristic $p>0$, the Frobenius map $x\mapsto x^p$ is an injective field endomorphism because
$$
(x+y)^p=x^p+y^p.
$$
Consequently every element of an extension field has at most one $p$th root.

= Formal derivative
{title2=$f'(T)$}
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

The formal derivative of $f(T)=\sum_i a_iT^i$ is $f'(T)=\sum_i ia_iT^{i-1}$.

= Formal derivative in positive characteristic
{title2=$f'(T)$}
{parent=Formal derivative}

In <characteristic of a field>[characteristic] $p$, the coefficient $i$ in a <formal derivative> vanishes exactly when $p$ divides $i$.

= Polynomial with zero formal derivative in characteristic p
{parent=Formal derivative in positive characteristic}

Over a field of characteristic $p>0$,
$$
f'(T)=0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
f(T)=g(T^p)
$$
for some polynomial $g$. Indeed, zero derivative says every exponent with nonzero coefficient is divisible by $p$.

= Separable polynomial
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki=Separable_polynomial}

A polynomial over a field is separable when it has no repeated root in a splitting field. Equivalently, it is coprime to its <formal derivative>.

= Separability of roots of unity
{parent=Separable polynomial}

If the <characteristic> of a field does not divide the <positive integer> $m$, then $X^m-1$ is <separable polynomial>[separable]. Its <formal derivative> is $mX^{m-1}$, and a common root would have to be both nonzero and zero.

= Irreducible polynomial in characteristic zero is separable
{parent=Separable polynomial}

If $f$ is irreducible over a field of characteristic zero, then $f'\ne0$ and $\deg f'<\deg f$. Thus $f$ cannot divide $f'$, so irreducibility gives $\gcd(f,f')=1$. Consequently $f$ has no repeated root in any extension field.

= Automorphism-count divisibility theorem
{parent=Galois theory}

For a finite extension $K/F$, the automorphism group $G=\operatorname{Aut}_F(K)$ has order dividing $[K:F]$. Indeed, Artin's theorem gives $[K:K^G]=|G|$, while $F\subseteq K^G$ and the tower law gives the divisibility.

= Coprime-degree descent for powers
{parent=Galois theory}

Let $L/K$ have degree $d$ with $\gcd(d,n)=1$. If $a=x^n\in K$ for some $x\in L$, then
$$
a^d=N_{L/K}(x)^n.
$$
Choosing $r,s\in\mathbb Z$ with $rd+sn=1$ gives
$$
a=\bigl(N_{L/K}(x)^r a^s\bigr)^n,
$$
so $a$ was already an $n$th power in $K$.

= Algebraic element
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

An element $\alpha$ of a field extension $L/K$ is algebraic over $K$ when some nonzero polynomial in $K[X]$ vanishes at $\alpha$.

= Minimal polynomial of an algebraic element
{title2=$m_{\alpha,K}(X)$}
{parent=Algebraic element}

The minimal polynomial of an algebraic element $\alpha$ over $K$ is the unique monic polynomial of least positive degree in $K[X]$ that vanishes at $\alpha$. It is irreducible, and its degree equals $[K(\alpha):K]$.

= Primitive element of a field extension
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki=Primitive_element}

A primitive element of a finite extension $L/K$ is an element $\alpha\in L$ such that $L=K(\alpha)$.

= Primitive elements form a principal Zariski-open set
{parent=Primitive element of a field extension}

Fix a $K$-basis of a finite extension $L/K$ of degree $n$. The coordinates of $1,\alpha,\ldots,\alpha^{n-1}$ are polynomial functions of the coordinates of $\alpha$, because multiplication in $L$ has fixed structure constants. The determinant $D(\alpha)$ of those coordinate columns is therefore a polynomial, and
$$
K[\alpha]=L\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad D(\alpha)\ne0.
$$
Thus the primitive-element locus is the distinguished <Zariski-open set> $D(D)\subseteq\mathbb A_K^n$, possibly empty for a non-simple extension.

= Primitive element theorem
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

Every finite separable extension $L/K$ is generated by one element: $L=K(\alpha)$ for some $\alpha\in L$.

= Finite separable simple extension implications
{parent=Galois theory}

A finite separable extension is simple by the primitive element theorem. A simple separable extension is finite because its generator is separable and hence algebraic. Finite and simple does not imply separable: in characteristic $p$, $\mathbb F_p(t^{1/p})/\mathbb F_p(t)$ is finite and simple but purely inseparable.

= Biquadratic extension
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

If $a,b,ab$ are nonsquares in a field $K$ of characteristic other than two, then
$$
K(\sqrt a,\sqrt b)/K
$$
is a degree-four Galois extension with group $C_2\times C_2$. Its three quadratic intermediate fields are $K(\sqrt a)$, $K(\sqrt b)$, and $K(\sqrt{ab})$.

= Transitive subgroups of the symmetric group on four points
{parent=Galois theory}

Up to conjugacy, the transitive subgroups of $S_4$ are $C_4$, $V_4$, $D_8$, $A_4$, and $S_4$.

= Dihedral Galois action on four radical roots
{parent=Galois theory}

If $r$ cyclically sends $\alpha\mapsto\beta\mapsto-\alpha\mapsto-\beta$ and $s$ fixes $\alpha$ while negating $\beta$, then $r^4=s^2=1$ and $srs=r^{-1}$. These automorphisms realize $D_8$ as the Galois group of the corresponding quartic splitting field.

= Galois group of an irreducible even quartic
{c}
{title2=$X^4+bX^2+c$}
{parent=Galois theory}

Let $g(X)=X^4+bX^2+c\in\mathbb Q[X]$ be irreducible, let $\alpha$ be a root, and put $\delta=\sqrt c$. Its roots are
$$
\alpha,-\alpha,\frac\delta\alpha,-\frac\delta\alpha,
$$
so its splitting field is $K=\mathbb Q(\alpha,\delta)$. If $\delta\notin\mathbb Q(\alpha)$, then $[K:\mathbb Q]=8$ and the Galois group is $D_8$. If $\delta\in\mathbb Q(\alpha)$, then $[K:\mathbb Q]=4$ and the group is $V_4$ when $\delta\in\mathbb Q$, and $C_4$ otherwise.

= Klein-four criterion for an irreducible even quartic
{c}
{parent=Galois group of an irreducible even quartic}

For irreducible $X^4+bX^2+c\in\mathbb Q[X]$, the Galois group is $C_2\times C_2$ exactly when $c$ is a square in $\mathbb Q$. In the transitive Klein-four action on the roots $\alpha,-\alpha,\beta,-\beta$, all three nonidentity double transpositions fix $\alpha\beta=\sqrt c$, forcing it into the fixed field $\mathbb Q$.

= Field homomorphism
{parent=Galois theory}
{wiki}

A field homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication, and the multiplicative identity.

= Field embedding
{parent=Field homomorphism}

A field embedding $L\to\Omega$ is an injective field homomorphism. A $K$-embedding fixes every element of the base field $K$.

= Linear independence of distinct field embeddings
{parent=Field embedding}
{wiki=Dedekind's_independence_of_characters}

= Artin independence theorem
{c}
{synonym}

Distinct field embeddings $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_n:L\to\Omega$ are linearly independent over $\Omega$ as functions on $L$. Thus a nontrivial linear combination $\sum_i a_i\sigma_i$ cannot vanish on every element of $L$.

For the proof, choose a vanishing relation with the fewest nonzero coefficients and normalize one coefficient to one. If $\sigma_1\ne\sigma_m$, choose $y$ with $\sigma_1(y)\ne\sigma_m(y)$.
