If $A$ is real symmetric, $s$ is not an eigenvalue, one eigenvalue $\lambda_*$ is uniquely closest to $s$, and the initial vector has a nonzero component in its eigenspace, fixed-shift inverse iteration converges to that eigenspace. Its asymptotic direction-error ratio is
$$
\frac{|\lambda_*-s|}{|\lambda_{\rm next}-s|},
$$
where $\lambda_{\rm next}$ is second closest to $s$.

= Sign behavior of fixed-shift inverse iteration
{parent=Convergence of fixed-shift inverse iteration}

If the closest eigenvalue $\lambda_*$ lies above the shift, the dominant eigenvalue $(\lambda_*-s)^{-1}$ of the inverse is positive and normalized iterates approach one fixed sign of its eigenvector. If $\lambda_*<s$, that inverse eigenvalue is negative, so the direction converges but consecutive normalized vectors alternate signs. At a shift exactly midway between two simple eigenvalues, the two inverse eigenvalues have equal modulus and opposite signs, so generic iterates alternate between two mixtures instead of selecting one eigenvector.

= Subspace iteration
{parent=Power method}
{wiki}

Subspace iteration repeatedly applies a matrix to several independent vectors and orthonormalizes them, converging to a dominant invariant subspace when a spectral gap separates the selected eigenvalue cluster.

= Dominant invariant subspace
{parent=Subspace iteration}

A dominant invariant subspace is spanned by eigenvectors whose eigenvalue moduli exceed those outside the subspace.

= Power method with a dominant eigenvalue cluster
{parent=Subspace iteration}

When several eigenvalues share the largest modulus, normalized power iterates need not converge individually, but every limit lies in their joint invariant subspace.

= Characteristic polynomial
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

The characteristic polynomial is
$$
\chi_A(t)=\det(tI-A).
$$
Its roots are precisely the eigenvalues of $A$.

= Characteristic polynomials of AB and BA
{title2=$\chi_{AB}=\chi_{BA}$}
{c}
{parent=Characteristic polynomial}

For square matrices $A,B$ over a field,
$$
\det(tI-AB)=\det(tI-BA).
$$
If $A$ is invertible, the products are <similar matrices>. In general, apply that case to $(A-sI)B$ and $B(A-sI)$ for all but finitely many $s$, then use polynomial identity in $s$.

= Faddeev–LeVerrier algorithm
{c}
{parent=Characteristic polynomial}
{wiki}

If
$$
\det(tI-A)=t^n+c_1t^{n-1}+\cdots+c_n,
$$
the Faddeev–LeVerrier algorithm computes the coefficients recursively from traces of powers:
$$
c_k=-\frac1k\left(\operatorname{tr}(A^k)+c_1\operatorname{tr}(A^{k-1})+\cdots+c_{k-1}\operatorname{tr}(A)\right).
$$

= Real parameter avoiding a singular matrix pencil
{parent=Characteristic polynomial}

For real matrices $A,B$, $p(t)=\det(A+tB)$ is a real polynomial. If $p(i)\ne0$, it is not the zero polynomial, so some real $\lambda$ satisfies $p(\lambda)\ne0$.

= Triangularization over an algebraically closed field
{parent=Characteristic polynomial}

Every square matrix over an algebraically closed field is similar to an upper-triangular matrix. Choose an eigenvector, extend it to a basis, and apply induction to the induced lower-right block.

= Eigenvalue interlacing
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

Eigenvalue interlacing bounds the ordered eigenvalues of a compressed or rank-modified operator between those of the original operator.

= Jordan normal form
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{c}
{wiki}

= Algebraic multiplicity
{parent=Jordan normal form}
{wiki}

The algebraic multiplicity of an eigenvalue is its multiplicity as a root of the characteristic polynomial. In Jordan form it is the sum of the sizes of all blocks carrying that eigenvalue.

= Geometric multiplicity
{parent=Jordan normal form}
{wiki}

The geometric multiplicity of an eigenvalue is the dimension of its eigenspace. In Jordan form it equals the number of blocks carrying that eigenvalue.

= Jordan block
{parent=Jordan normal form}
{c}
{wiki}

A Jordan block has one eigenvalue on its diagonal and ones on its superdiagonal.

= Nilpotent Jordan block
{title2=$J_d(0)$}
{parent=Jordan block}
{c}

A nilpotent Jordan block has zeros on its diagonal and ones on its superdiagonal. Its $k$th power has ones on the $k$th superdiagonal, and a block of size $d$ satisfies $J_d(0)^d=0$.

= Jordan normal form of conjugation on two-by-two matrices
{parent=Jordan normal form}
{c}

If $\beta$ has eigenvalues $\lambda,\mu\ne0$ and is diagonalizable on a two-dimensional complex vector space, then
$$
\operatorname{JNF}(\phi_\beta)
=\operatorname{diag}\left(1,1,\frac\mu\lambda,\frac\lambda\mu\right).
$$
If $\beta$ has one size-two Jordan block, then
$$
\operatorname{JNF}(\phi_\beta)=J_3(1)\oplus J_1(1).
$$

= Characteristic and minimal polynomials determine similarity in dimension three
{parent=Jordan normal form}

Over an algebraically closed field, the characteristic polynomial gives the total size of the Jordan blocks for each eigenvalue, while the minimal polynomial gives the largest block size. For total dimension at most three, these two numbers determine the partition into block sizes: a multiplicity-three eigenvalue has partition $3$, $2+1$, or $1+1+1$ according as its largest block has size $3$, $2$, or $1$. Hence two matrices of size at most three with the same characteristic and minimal polynomials are similar.

= Generalized eigenvector
{parent=Jordan normal form}
{wiki}

A generalized eigenvector lies in the kernel of a positive power of A minus lambda I.

= Generalized eigenspace
{title2=$\ker(A-\lambda I)^N$}
{parent=Generalized eigenvector}
{wiki}

The generalized eigenspace of a <linear operator> $A$ for an <eigenvalue> $\lambda$ is the union of the kernels $\ker(A-\lambda I)^N$. In finite dimension this union stabilizes for sufficiently large $N$.

= Generalized eigenspaces for distinct eigenvalues form a direct sum
{parent=Generalized eigenvector}

For a linear operator $f$, the generalized eigenspaces
$$
\ker(f-\alpha I)^n
$$
belonging to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. On the generalized $\alpha$-eigenspace, $f-\beta I=(\alpha-\beta)I+N$ is invertible for $\beta\ne\alpha$ because the nilpotent part $N$ has a finite geometric-series inverse.

= Generalized eigenvalue problem
{title2=$Av=\lambda Bv$}
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A generalized eigenvalue problem asks for a nonzero vector $v$ and scalar $\lambda$ satisfying $Av=\lambda Bv$. When $B$ is invertible, it is equivalent to the ordinary <eigenvalue> problem $B^{-1}Av=\lambda v$.

= Generalized characteristic polynomial
{title2=$\det(A-\lambda B)$}
{parent=Generalized eigenvalue problem}

The generalized characteristic polynomial of a matrix pair $(A,B)$ is $\det(A-\lambda B)$. Every finite generalized eigenvalue is one of its roots.

= Matrix exponential
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

For a square matrix $A$, its exponential is the convergent power series
$$
e^{tA}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^kA^k}{k!}.
$$
It is the fundamental matrix for $y'=Ay$ and satisfies $e^{(s+t)A}=e^{sA}e^{tA}$.

= Baker--Campbell--Hausdorff formula
{parent=Matrix exponential}
{c}
{wiki=Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff_formula}

The Baker--Campbell--Hausdorff formula expresses the local <matrix logarithm> of a product of exponentials as
$$
\log(e^Xe^Y)=X+Y+\frac12[X,Y]+\frac1{12}[X,[X,Y]]+\frac1{12}[Y,[Y,X]]+\cdots.
$$

= Laplace transform of a matrix exponential
{parent=Matrix exponential}

On its half-plane of convergence,
$$
\mathcal L\{e^{tA}\}(s)=(sI-A)^{-1}.
$$
Indeed, applying the transform to $y'=Ay$, $y(0)=y_0$, gives $(sI-A)Y=y_0$ for every $y_0$.

= Minimal polynomial
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

The minimal polynomial $m_A$ is the unique monic polynomial of least degree satisfying $m_A(A)=0$. It divides every polynomial that annihilates $A$.

= Minimal polynomial of an invertible matrix
{parent=Minimal polynomial}

A square matrix $A$ is <invertible matrix>[invertible] exactly when its <minimal polynomial> has nonzero constant term. If $m_A(t)=tq(t)$, then $Aq(A)=0$, and invertibility would contradict the minimality of $m_A$. Conversely, if $m_A(t)=tq(t)+c$ with $c\ne0$, then $A[-q(A)/c]=I$.

= Minimal polynomials of AB and BA
{title2=$m_{AB},m_{BA}$}
{c}
{parent=Minimal polynomial}

For square matrices $A,B$,
$$
m_{BA}(t)\mid t,m_{AB}(t),
\qquad
m_{AB}(t)\mid t,m_{BA}(t).
$$
Indeed, $(BA)^{k+1}=B(AB)^kA$. Thus the two minimal polynomials have the same nonzero factors with the same multiplicities, while their powers of $t$ differ by at most one.

= Kernel decomposition for coprime polynomials
{parent=Minimal polynomial}

If $p_1,\ldots,p_r$ are pairwise coprime, then
$$
\ker\!\left(\prod_i p_i(A)\right)
=\bigoplus_i\ker p_i(A).
$$
Bezout identities between the factors construct the corresponding projections.

= Minimal polynomial bound from a triangular invariant flag
{parent=Minimal polynomial}

If an upper-triangular $n$ by $n$ matrix has diagonal entries $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n$, its standard invariant flag satisfies
$$
(A-\lambda_kI)V_k\subseteq V_{k-1}.
$$
The commuting product $\prod_k(A-\lambda_kI)$ therefore vanishes, proving $\deg m_A\leq n$ without invoking the full Cayley-Hamilton theorem.

= Jacobson lemma for a commuting commutator
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{c}

Let $C=[B,A]$ and suppose $AC=CA$. Then $C$ is nilpotent. For the derivation $D(X)=[B,X]$, one has $D(p(A))=p'(A)C$. If $f(A)=0$, induction gives
$$
f^{(k)}(A)C^{2^k-1}=0.
$$
Indeed, if $uC^m=C^mu=0$, differentiating $uC^m=0$ and multiplying on the left by $C^m$ gives $C^mD(u)C^m=0$; for $u=f^{(k)}(A)$ this replaces $m$ by $2m+1$. Taking $k=\deg f$ makes $f^{(k)}$ a nonzero constant and proves nilpotence.

= Translation finite-difference operator
{parent=Linear operator theory}

On all real-valued functions on $\mathbb R$, define $D_rf(x)=f(x+r)-f(x)$. The operators commute, and every real number except $-1$ is an eigenvalue. For eigenvalue $\lambda\ne-1$, values may be chosen independently on the cosets of $r\mathbb Z$ and extended by $f(x+r)=(1+\lambda)f(x)$, so every eigenspace is infinite-dimensional.

= Mixed finite difference of a polynomial
{parent=Translation finite-difference operator}

If $p$ has degree $n$ and leading coefficient $a_n$, then for nonzero steps $r_1,\ldots,r_n$,
$$
D_{r_1}\cdots D_{r_n}p=n!a_n\prod_{i=1}^nr_i.
$$
Consequently a degree-$n$ polynomial cannot be the sum of $n$ functions each periodic with some nonzero period.

= Nilpotent linear map
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

= Nilpotent operator
{synonym}

A <linear map> $T:V\to V$ is nilpotent if $T^k=0$ for some <positive integer> $k$.

= Eigenvalue
{title2=$\lambda$}
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A <scalar> $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of a <linear operator> $A$ when $Av=\lambda v$ for some nonzero <vector> $v$.

= Zero eigenvalue
{title2=$\lambda=0$}
{parent=Eigenvalue}

Zero is an eigenvalue of a linear operator exactly when the operator has a nontrivial <kernel>. Its eigenvectors are precisely the nonzero vectors in that kernel.

= Eigenvalue problem
{parent=Eigenvalue}

An eigenvalue problem asks for the <eigenvalues> and corresponding <eigenvectors> or <eigenfunctions> of a <linear operator>. It is commonly written as the <eigenvalue equation> $Av=\lambda v$.

= Eigenfunction
{parent=Eigenvalue}
{wiki}

An eigenfunction is a nonzero function $f$ satisfying $Lf=\lambda f$ for a linear operator $L$ and scalar <eigenvalue> $\lambda$.

= Eigenvector
{title2=$v$}
{parent=Eigenvalue}
{wiki}

A nonzero <vector> $v$ is an eigenvector of a <linear operator> $A$ when $Av=\lambda v$ for some <eigenvalue> $\lambda$.

= Eigenvalue equation
{title2=$Av=\lambda v$}
{parent=Eigenvector}

The eigenvalue equation $Av=\lambda v$ asks for a nonzero <eigenvector> $v$ and its <eigenvalue> $\lambda$.

= Right eigenvector
{title2=$v$}
{parent=Eigenvector}

A right eigenvector satisfies $Av=\lambda v$.

= Left eigenvector
{title2=$u$}
{parent=Eigenvector}

A left eigenvector satisfies $u^TA=\lambda u^T$, equivalently $A^Tu=\lambda u$ over the real numbers.

= Eigenspace
{parent=Eigenvalue}
{wiki}

The eigenspace for $\lambda$ is $\ker(A-\lambda I)$.

= Eigenbasis
{parent=Eigenspace}
{wiki=Eigenbasis}

An eigenbasis is a <basis> consisting of <eigenvectors> of one operator.

= Simple eigenvalue
{parent=Eigenvalue}

An eigenvalue is simple when it has <algebraic multiplicity> one.

= Eigenvalue sensitivity
{title2=$s(\lambda)$}
{parent=Simple eigenvalue}

For unit <left eigenvector> $u$ and <right eigenvector> $v$ of a real matrix at a <simple eigenvalue> $\lambda$, its normwise sensitivity is
$$
s(\lambda)=\frac1{|u^Tv|}.
$$
It measures the first-order amplification of a matrix perturbation into an eigenvalue perturbation.

= First-order perturbation of a simple eigenvalue
{parent=Eigenvalue sensitivity}

If $A(t)=A+tE$ and $\lambda(t)$ is a <differentiable function> defining a simple eigenvalue, then
$$
\lambda'(0)=\frac{u^TEv}{u^Tv}.
$$
The <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> and the <operator norm> give
$$
|\lambda'(0)|\leq\|E\|_2s(\lambda).
$$

= Diagonalizable matrix
{parent=Eigenvalue}
{wiki=Diagonalizable_matrix}

A matrix is diagonalizable exactly when its eigenvectors span the whole vector space.

= Diagonalization of a matrix
{parent=Diagonalizable matrix}

A diagonalization is a factorization $A=PDP^{-1}$ in which $D$ is diagonal and the columns of $P$ form an eigenvector basis.

= Diagonalizability inherited from an invertible power
{parent=Diagonalizable matrix}

Let $A$ be invertible over an algebraically closed field. If $A^m$ is diagonalizable for some $m>0$, then $A$ is diagonalizable. Indeed, a nontrivial Jordan block $A|_E=\lambda I+N$ has $\lambda\ne0$ and
$$
(\lambda I+N)^m=\lambda^mI+m\lambda^{m-1}N+\cdots.
$$
The first nonzero nilpotent term prevents this power from being diagonalizable.

= Complex similarity of real matrices implies real similarity
{parent=Diagonalizable matrix}

Suppose real matrices $A,B$ satisfy $AP=PB$ for an invertible complex matrix $P=X+iY$. Then $AX=XB$ and $AY=YB$. The real polynomial $\det(X+tY)$ is not identically zero because its value at $t=i$ is nonzero, so some real $t$ makes $X+tY$ invertible. This real matrix intertwines $A$ and $B$.

= Distinct eigenvalues imply diagonalizability
{parent=Diagonalizable matrix}

Eigenvectors belonging to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. Consequently, an $n$-dimensional operator with $n$ distinct eigenvalues is diagonalizable.

= Spectral decomposition
{parent=Diagonalizable matrix}
{wiki}

= Eigendecomposition
{synonym}

A spectral decomposition expresses a <linear operator> through its <eigenvalues> and projections onto its <eigenspaces>.

= Dominant eigenvalue
{parent=Spectral decomposition}
{wiki}

An <eigenvalue> is dominant when its <modulus> is greater than or equal to that of every other eigenvalue. It is uniquely dominant when the inequality is strict.

= Spectral gap
{parent=Spectral decomposition}
{wiki}

A spectral gap is a positive separation between designated parts of the <spectrum> of a <linear operator>. For the <power method>, the relevant gap separates the modulus of the <dominant eigenvalue> from the other eigenvalue moduli.

= Adjoint matrix
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki=Conjugate_transpose}

The adjoint of a complex matrix is its conjugate transpose,
$$
A^\dagger=\overline A^T.
$$

= Unitary matrix
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A complex matrix is unitary when $U^\dagger U=UU^\dagger=I$. Its columns form an orthonormal basis, and it preserves inner products and norms.

= Skew-Hermitian matrix
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

= Skew-Hermitian
{synonym}
{c}

A complex matrix $A$ is skew-Hermitian when $A^\dagger=-A$. Its <matrix exponential> $e^{tA}$ is <unitary matrix>[unitary] for every real $t$.

= Cayley transform of a Hermitian matrix
{title2=$(I+iA)^{-1}(I-iA)$}
{parent=Linear operator theory}

If $A$ is a <Hermitian matrix>, then $(I+iA)^{-1}(I-iA)$ is <unitary matrix>[unitary]. This Cayley transform maps each real eigenvalue $\lambda$ to the unit-modulus number $(1-i\lambda)/(1+i\lambda)$.

= Normal matrix
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A matrix is normal when $AA^\dagger=A^\dagger A$.

= Matrix 2-norm of a normal matrix
{parent=Normal matrix}

For every finite-dimensional normal matrix $A$, the <matrix 2-norm> equals the <spectral radius>:
$$
\lVert A\rVert_2=\max_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}|\lambda|=\rho(A).
$$
This follows by <unitary diagonalization of a normal matrix> and unitary invariance of the matrix 2-norm.

= Non-normal matrix
{parent=Normal matrix}

A matrix is non-normal when it does not commute with its adjoint. Its eigenvectors need not be orthogonal, so combinations of individually decaying modes may grow temporarily.

= Transient growth
{parent=Non-normal matrix}

Transient growth is finite-time amplification in a stable linear system. For a non-normal generator it can occur even when every eigenvalue has negative real part, because nonorthogonal eigenmodes can interfere constructively.

= Unitary diagonalization of a normal matrix
{parent=Normal matrix}

Every finite-dimensional complex normal matrix has an orthonormal eigenbasis, equivalently
$$
U^\dagger AU=D
$$
for some unitary $U$ and diagonal $D$.

= Schur triangularization
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{c}
{wiki=Schur_decomposition}

Every complex square matrix is unitarily similar to an upper triangular matrix. The proof selects an eigenvector, extends it to an orthonormal basis, and applies induction to the compression on its orthogonal complement.

= Unilateral shift operator
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki=Shift_operator}

The unilateral shifts on sequences move every component one place left or right. Their spectra illustrate behavior absent in finite dimensions.

= Point spectrum
{title2=$\sigma_p(T)$}
{parent=Unilateral shift operator}

The point spectrum of an operator is its set of eigenvalues.

= Wandering-vector characterization of a unilateral shift
{parent=Unilateral shift operator}

An operator $T$ is a unilateral shift exactly when it is an isometry, $(\operatorname{Im}T)^\perp$ is one-dimensional, and $\bigcap_{n\geq1}\operatorname{Im}(T^n)=\{0\}$. A unit vector $e_1$ in the first orthogonal complement is wandering, and $e_n=T^{n-1}e_1$ is a complete orthonormal basis.

= Rayleigh quotient
{title2=$R[\psi]$}
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{c}
{wiki}

For a self-adjoint operator $H$, the Rayleigh quotient is
$$
R[\psi]=\frac{\langle\psi|H|\psi\rangle}{\langle\psi|\psi\rangle}.
$$
For a symmetric matrix this reduces to $x^TAx/(x^Tx)$.

= Generalized Rayleigh quotient
{title2=$\dfrac{x^TAx}{x^TBx}$}
{parent=Rayleigh quotient}

For symmetric $A$ and positive-definite $B$, the generalized Rayleigh quotient is $x^TAx/(x^TBx)$. Its stationary values are the <generalized eigenvalue problem>[generalized eigenvalues] of $Av=\lambda Bv$, and its maximum is the largest such eigenvalue.

= Rayleigh quotient iteration
{parent=Rayleigh quotient}
{c}
{wiki}

Starting from a unit vector $v_k$, Rayleigh quotient iteration sets $\mu_k=R[v_k]$, solves
$$
(A-\mu_kI)w_k=v_k,
$$
and normalizes $v_{k+1}=w_k/\|w_k\|_2$. The shift $\mu_k$ is updated from the new vector.

= Local cubic convergence of Rayleigh quotient iteration
{parent=Rayleigh quotient iteration}
{c}

Let a real symmetric matrix have simple eigenpairs $(\lambda_i,w_i)$ and let
$$
x=c^{-1}\left(w_1+\epsilon\sum_{i\geq2}a_iw_i\right).
$$
Then its <Rayleigh quotient> is
$$
R_A(x)=\lambda_1+\epsilon^2D+O(\epsilon^4),
\qquad
D=\sum_{i\geq2}a_i^2(\lambda_i-\lambda_1).
$$
After one shifted inverse solve and normalization, the relative coefficients are
$$
-\epsilon^3\frac{Da_i}{\lambda_i-\lambda_1}+O(\epsilon^5).
$$
Thus the eigenvector direction error is cubed at each sufficiently close iterate.

= Rayleigh-Ritz variational principle
{parent=Rayleigh quotient}
{c}

The extremal Rayleigh quotients of a self-adjoint operator are the extremal spectral values. In particular, a Hamiltonian with a discrete spectrum satisfies $R[\psi]\geq E_0$, with equality precisely on its ground-state eigenspace. More generally, minimizing over states orthogonal to the first $j$ eigenspaces gives an upper bound on $E_j$.

= Odd-state variational principle for an even potential
{parent=Rayleigh-Ritz variational principle}

For a one-dimensional even potential, the ground state is even and the first excited state is odd. Every normalized odd trial wavefunction is therefore orthogonal to the ground state and gives
$$
E_1\leq\langle\psi_{\rm odd},H\psi_{\rm odd}\rangle.
$$

= Gaussian variational bound for an attractive Gaussian well
{c}
{parent=Rayleigh-Ritz variational principle}

For
$$
H=-\frac{d^2}{dx^2}-V_0e^{-x^2}
$$
and the trial state $\psi_a(x)=e^{-ax^2/2}$,
$$
E_0\leq E(a)
=\frac a2-V_0\sqrt{\frac a{1+a}}.
$$
This is negative for some $a>0$ for every $V_0>0$, proving that the attractive well has a bound state. For small $V_0$, taking $a=V_0^2$ gives
$$
-V_0<E_0\leq-\frac12V_0^2+O(V_0^4).
$$

= Finite-subspace variational method
{parent=Rayleigh-Ritz variational principle}

For an orthonormal trial set $\phi_1,\ldots,\phi_N$, the Rayleigh quotient on their span is
$$
\frac{\alpha^\dagger\mathcal H\alpha}{\alpha^\dagger\alpha},
\qquad
\mathcal H_{nm}=\langle\phi_n|H|\phi_m\rangle.
$$
Its minimum is the smallest eigenvalue of $\mathcal H$, which is therefore the optimal variational upper bound available in that trial subspace.

= Two-mode variational bound for a linearly tilted square well
{parent=Finite-subspace variational method}

For the first two sine modes in a width-$a$ infinite well with
$V(x)=9\hbar^2x/(ma^3)$, the Hamiltonian in units $\hbar^2/(ma^2)$ is
$$
\begin{pmatrix}
(\pi^2+9)/2&-16/\pi^2\\
-16/\pi^2&(4\pi^2+9)/2
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Its lower eigenvalue gives the upper bound
$$
E_0\leq\frac{\hbar^2}{ma^2}
\left[
\frac{5\pi^2+18}{4}
-\sqrt{\frac{9\pi^4}{16}+\frac{256}{\pi^4}}
\right].
$$

= Cyclic vector
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A cyclic vector has iterates under an operator that span the whole vector space.

= Companion matrix
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

A companion matrix represents multiplication by x in the power basis modulo a monic polynomial.

= Invariant direct-sum decomposition
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

An invariant direct-sum decomposition splits a space into nonzero subspaces preserved by the operator.

= Rational canonical form
{parent=Linear operator theory}
{wiki}

Rational canonical form expresses a linear map as a direct sum of companion matrices of invariant factors, and works over any field.

= Invariant factors of a linear operator
{parent=Rational canonical form}

For a finite-dimensional vector space regarded as an $F[X]$-module through a linear operator, the monic invariant factors $a_1\mid\cdots\mid a_k$ give the decomposition $\bigoplus_iF[X]/(a_i)$. Their product is the characteristic polynomial and the largest is the minimal polynomial.

= Multilinear algebra
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

Multilinear algebra studies maps linear in each of several arguments, including determinants, tensors, and exterior products.

= Alternating multilinear map
{parent=Multilinear algebra}

An alternating multilinear map vanishes whenever two arguments are equal. Swapping two arguments reverses its sign.

= Tensor product
{title2=$\otimes$}
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

The tensor product $V\otimes W$ of <vector spaces> is generated by symbols $v\otimes w$ subject to bilinearity. It is characterized by the property that every bilinear map from $V\times W$ factors uniquely through a <linear map> from $V\otimes W$.

= Tensor algebra
{title2=$T(V)$}
{parent=Tensor product}
{wiki}

The tensor algebra of a <vector space> $V$ is the <graded algebra>
$$
T(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}V^{\otimes n},
$$
with multiplication given by concatenation of tensors.

= Tensor power
{title2=$V^{\otimes n}$}
{parent=Tensor algebra}

The nth tensor power is the iterated <tensor product of modules> $V^{\otimes n}=V\otimes\cdots\otimes V$.

= Tensor square
{title2=$V\otimes V$}
{parent=Tensor power}

The tensor square is the second <tensor power> $V^{\otimes2}=V\otimes V$. Over a field of characteristic different from two it decomposes as the <direct sum> of the <symmetric square> and <exterior square>.

= Symmetric algebra
{title2=$S(V)$}
{parent=Tensor algebra}
{wiki}

The symmetric algebra is the quotient of $T(V)$ by the ideal generated by $v\otimes w-w\otimes v$. It is graded by <symmetric powers>:
$$
S(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}S^nV.
$$

= Symmetric power
{title2=$S^nV$}
{parent=Symmetric algebra}
{wiki}

The nth symmetric power $S^nV$ is the quotient of $V^{\otimes n}$ that identifies tensors differing by a permutation of their factors. Equivalently, over characteristic zero it is the subspace of symmetric tensors.

= Symmetric square
{title2=$S^2V$}
{parent=Symmetric power}

The symmetric square is the quotient of $V\otimes V$ by $v\otimes w-w\otimes v$, and records symmetric tensors of rank two.

= Exterior algebra
{title2=$\Lambda^\bullet V$}
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

The exterior algebra is the graded algebra
$$
\Lambda^\bullet V=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\dim V}\Lambda^kV
$$
with multiplication given by the antisymmetric wedge product.

= Grassmann algebra
{parent=Exterior algebra}
{wiki}

A Grassmann algebra is an exterior algebra regarded as an algebra of anticommuting generators. Its odd elements satisfy $\theta_i\theta_j=-\theta_j\theta_i$.

= Grassmann variable
{parent=Grassmann algebra}

A Grassmann variable is an odd generator $\theta$ of a <Grassmann algebra>. In characteristic zero it obeys $\theta^2=0$, so functions of finitely many Grassmann variables have finite Taylor expansions.

= Exterior power
{title2=$\Lambda^kV$}
{parent=Exterior algebra}

The $k$th exterior power $\Lambda^kV$ is the quotient of $V^{\otimes k}$ that makes every tensor with two equal factors zero. It represents alternating $k$-linear maps and has dimension $\binom{\dim V}{k}$ in finite dimensions.

= Exterior square
{title2=$\bigwedge^2V$}
{parent=Exterior power}

The exterior square is the quotient of $V\otimes V$ by tensors $v\otimes v$, and records antisymmetric tensors of rank two.

= Tensor
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

A rank-$n$ tensor on a vector space $V$ is a multilinear map of $n$ vector or covector arguments, equivalently an element of an $n$-fold tensor product. Its components transform by one copy of the change-of-basis matrix for each index.

= Slice rank
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki}

For finite sets $X,Y,Z$ and a field $F$, a three-variable tensor $T:X\times Y\times Z\to F$ has slice rank at most $r$ when it is a sum of $r$ terms, each of which separates one variable from the other two. Such terms have one of the forms
$$
a(x)b(y,z),\qquad c(y)d(x,z),\qquad e(z)h(x,y).
$$
The slice rank is the least possible $r$.

= Diagonal tensor
{parent=Slice rank}

A three-variable tensor is diagonal when it vanishes unless its three arguments are equal.

= Slice rank of a diagonal tensor
{parent=Diagonal tensor}

If
$$
T(x,y,z)=\begin{cases}c_x&x=y=z,\\0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
$$
on a finite set $X$, and every $c_x$ is nonzero, then the <slice rank> of $T$ is $|X|$. The upper bound uses one $x$-slice for each diagonal entry. For the lower bound, restrict any shorter slice decomposition to a common kernel of the coefficient functions in two slice directions; the remaining diagonal matrix has rank larger than the number of slices available in the third direction, a contradiction.

= Covariance and contravariance of vectors
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki}

Covariance and contravariance describe how vector and covector components transform when a basis changes.

= Covariant tensor
{parent=Covariance and contravariance of vectors}

A covariant tensor is multilinear in vector arguments and transforms with one covariant index for each argument.

= Kronecker delta
{title2=$\delta_{ij}$}
{c}
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki}

The Kronecker delta is $\delta_{ij}=1$ when $i=j$ and zero otherwise. It is the component matrix of the identity map in a basis.

= Symmetric second-rank tensor
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki=Symmetric_tensor}

A second-rank tensor is symmetric when $T_{ij}=T_{ji}$. Every second-rank tensor has the unique decomposition
$$
T_{ij}=\frac{T_{ij}+T_{ji}}2+\frac{T_{ij}-T_{ji}}2
$$
into symmetric and antisymmetric parts.

= Antisymmetric second-rank tensor
{parent=Tensor}

A second-rank Cartesian tensor is antisymmetric when its components form a <skew-symmetric matrix>, $T_{ij}=-T_{ji}$. Orthogonal coordinate changes preserve this property because $T'=RTR^T$ implies $(T')^T=RT^TR^T=-T'$.

= Totally antisymmetric tensor
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki=Antisymmetric_tensor}

A tensor changes sign whenever two indices are interchanged. In three dimensions, a rank-two antisymmetric tensor is $A_{ij}=a_k\epsilon_{kij}$, a rank-three one is $A_{ijk}=a\epsilon_{ijk}$, and every totally antisymmetric tensor of rank greater than three vanishes.

= Isotropic tensor
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki}

An isotropic tensor is invariant under every proper orthogonal change of basis. A rank-four isotropic tensor has the form
$$
T_{ijkl}=a\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}
+b\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}
+c\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}.
$$
Rank-five isotropic pseudotensors are formed by multiplying one Kronecker delta by one Levi-Civita symbol and permuting the five indices.

= Einstein notation
{parent=Tensor}
{c}
{wiki}

= Einstein summation convention
{c}
{synonym}

= Suffix notation
{synonym}

Einstein notation sums automatically over an index repeated once up and once down, or twice in Euclidean coordinates.

= Tensor contraction
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

= Metric trace
{parent=Tensor contraction}

The metric trace contracts two indices of a covariant rank-two tensor: $T=g^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}$. For the metric itself in $D$ dimensions, $g^{\mu\nu}g_{\mu\nu}=D$.

= Reciprocal basis
{parent=Multilinear algebra}

For a basis $e_1,e_2,e_3$ with <scalar triple product> $\Delta$, the reciprocal vectors are cyclic cross products divided by $\Delta$ and satisfy $f_i\cdot e_j=\delta_{ij}$. This construction is used for the basis of a <reciprocal lattice>.

= Scalar triple product
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

The scalar triple product $a\cdot(b\times c)$ is the signed volume of the parallelepiped spanned by three vectors.

= Orientation of a vector space
{parent=Multilinear algebra}

An orientation of a finite-dimensional real vector space distinguishes its ordered bases into two classes according to the sign of their change-of-basis determinant.

= Oriented volume
{parent=Orientation of a vector space}

An oriented volume retains the sign of a determinant; reversing the orientation changes that sign.

= Determinant
{title2=$\det$}
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

The determinant is the alternating multilinear volume scale of a square matrix; it is nonzero exactly for invertible matrices.

= Log-determinant
{title2=$\log\det A$}
{parent=Determinant}

On <positive-definite matrices>, the log-determinant has differential $d\log\det A=\operatorname{Tr}(A^{-1}dA)$ and is a <strictly concave function>.

= Multilinearity of the determinant
{parent=Determinant}

The determinant is linear in each row and in each column when all the others are held fixed.

= Multiplicativity of the determinant
{title2=$\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$}
{parent=Determinant}

The determinant is multiplicative: for square matrices of the same size, $\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$.

= Leibniz formula for determinants
{parent=Determinant}
{c}
{wiki=Leibniz_formula_for_determinants}

For an $n$ by $n$ matrix,
$$
\det A=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)
\prod_{i=1}^na_{i,\sigma(i)}.
$$

= Tridiagonal determinant recurrence
{parent=Determinant}

For diagonal entries $a_i$, superdiagonal entries $b_i$, and subdiagonal entries $c_i$, the leading principal determinants satisfy
$$
d_n=a_nd_{n-1}-b_{n-1}c_{n-1}d_{n-2},
\qquad d_0=1,\quad d_1=a_1.
$$

= System of linear equations
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Linear system
{synonym}

A linear system $Ax=b$ is consistent when $b$ lies in the column space of $A$; its solutions form a translate of $\ker A$.

= Linear equation
{parent=System of linear equations}
{wiki}

A linear equation is an equation in which the unknowns occur only to the first power and are combined linearly.

= Fredholm alternative for a matrix
{parent=System of linear equations}
{c}

The system $Ax=b$ is solvable exactly when $b$ is orthogonal to every vector in $\ker A^T$.

= Symmetric and antisymmetric parts of a matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}

Every square matrix decomposes uniquely as $A=(A+A^T)/2+(A-A^T)/2$, a symmetric part plus an antisymmetric part.

= Symmetric matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A real matrix is symmetric when $A^T=A$. It represents a <self-adjoint operator> in the standard Euclidean <inner product>.

= Spectral theorem for real symmetric matrices
{parent=Symmetric matrix}
{wiki=Spectral_theorem}

Every real symmetric matrix has an <orthonormal basis> of real eigenvectors. Equivalently, it admits an orthogonal diagonalization $A=O^TDO$ with $D$ real diagonal and $O$ an <orthogonal matrix>.

= Axis-angle decomposition
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki=Axis–angle_representation}

An axis-angle map fixes or scales one axis and acts on its perpendicular plane by a rotation, possibly combined with a dilation.

= Inverse of an axis-angle linear map
{parent=Axis-angle decomposition}

Invert the axial scalar and invert the perpendicular complex scalar $\alpha+i\gamma$ as $(\alpha-i\gamma)/(\alpha^2+\gamma^2)$.

= Reflection
{disambiguate=mathematics}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Reflection in mathematics
{synonym}

A reflection fixes a hyperplane pointwise and reverses its normal direction.

= Reflection matrix
{parent=Reflection (mathematics)}

A plane reflection with unit normal $n$ is $I-2nn^T$; it fixes the plane $n^\perp$ and negates $n$.

= Reflection in a hyperplane
{parent=Reflection matrix}

= Orthogonal reflection
{synonym}

For a nonzero normal vector $a$, reflection in $a\cdot x=0$ has matrix
$$
I-\frac{2aa^T}{a^Ta}.
$$
Its eigenvalues are $-1$ along $a$ and $1$ on $a^\perp$, so its determinant is $-1$.

= Composition of two plane reflections
{parent=Reflection matrix}

The composition of reflections in two planes through the origin is a rotation about their line of intersection through twice the oriented angle between the planes.

= Rotation matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A rotation matrix is an orthogonal matrix with determinant $1$.

= Rotational symmetry
{parent=Rotation matrix}
{wiki}

An object has rotational symmetry when a nontrivial <rotation matrix> leaves it unchanged.

= Cartesian coordinate system
{parent=Linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

A Cartesian coordinate system specifies a point by its signed components along mutually perpendicular coordinate axes.

= Positive quadrant
{parent=Cartesian coordinate system}
{wiki=Quadrant_(plane_geometry)}

The positive quadrant of the Cartesian plane is the set of points $(x,y)$ with $x>0$ and $y>0$.

= Matrix geometric series
{parent=Linear algebra}

For invertible $I-A$, $\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}A^j=(I-A^n)(I-A)^{-1}$.

= Adjugate matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

The adjugate is the transpose of the cofactor matrix and satisfies $A\operatorname{adj}(A)=(\det A)I$.

= Adjugate identity
{parent=Adjugate matrix}

Every square matrix $M$ over a commutative ring satisfies
$$
M\operatorname{adj}(M)=\operatorname{adj}(M)M=\det(M)I.
$$

= Matrix equivalence
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

Equivalent matrices represent the same linear map after independent changes of domain and codomain bases.

= Rank normal form
{parent=Matrix equivalence}

Every matrix of rank $r$ is equivalent to a block matrix with $I_r$ in its upper-left corner and zeros elsewhere. This follows by choosing bases adapted to the kernel and image of the represented <linear map>.

= Block upper triangular matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}

A block upper triangular matrix is a <block matrix> with square diagonal blocks and zero blocks below them. Its determinant is the product of the determinants of its diagonal blocks.

= Eigenvalue deflation by an invariant subspace
{parent=Block upper triangular matrix}

If a $k$-dimensional <invariant subspace> is mapped to the span of the first $k$ coordinate vectors by a <similarity transformation>, the transformed matrix has block form
$$
\begin{pmatrix}B&C\\0&D\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Its <characteristic polynomial> factors as $\det(\lambda I-B)\det(\lambda I-D)$, so its eigenvalues are the eigenvalues of the two diagonal blocks, counted with <algebraic multiplicity>.

= Sylvester equation
{c}
{title2=$AX-XB=C$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

The Sylvester equation $AX-XB=C$ has a unique solution $X$ for every $C$ exactly when $A$ and $B$ have disjoint spectra.

= Rank inequality for a composition
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

For maps through a finite-dimensional intermediate space V, rank(alpha beta) is at least rank(alpha)+rank(beta)-dim(V).

= Schur complement
{parent=Linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

For an invertible block P, the Schur complement of P in a block matrix is $S-RP^{-1}Q$.

= Orthogonal similarity
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

Orthogonal similarity maps A to QAQ^T and preserves eigenvalues and symmetry.

= Product of symmetric matrices
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

Every complex square matrix is a product of two symmetric matrices, one invertible.

= Inner product
{title2=$\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Dot product
{title2=$\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b$}
{parent=Inner product}
{wiki}

The dot product of Euclidean vectors is $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=\sum_i a_i b_i$. It equals $|\mathbf a||\mathbf b|\cos\theta$, where $\theta$ is the angle between them.

= Binary inner product
{title2=$x\cdot y$}
{parent=Inner product}

For $x,y\in\mathbb F_2^n$, the binary inner product is $x\cdot y=\sum_i x_iy_i$ in $\mathbb F_2$. Its value is the parity of the coordinates at which both vectors are one.

= Inner-product space
{parent=Inner product}
{wiki}

An inner-product space is a <vector space> equipped with an <inner product>. The inner product induces the <normed vector space>[norm] $\|x\|=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}$ and hence a <metric space>[metric].

= Orthonormal set
{parent=Inner product}

An orthonormal set consists of <vectors> that have <normed vector space>[norm] one and are pairwise <orthogonal>. Its <Gram matrix> is the <identity matrix>.

= Orthonormal basis
{parent=Orthonormal set}
{wiki}

An orthonormal basis is a <basis> whose vectors are pairwise <orthogonal vectors>[orthogonal] and each have <normed vector space>[norm] one.

= Gram matrix
{parent=Inner product}
{c}
{wiki}

For vectors $v_1,\ldots,v_n$, the Gram matrix is
$$
G_{ij}=\langle v_i,v_j\rangle.
$$
Two finite vector families with the same Gram matrix are related by an isometry between their spans; in finite dimensions that isometry can be extended to a unitary map after completing orthonormal bases.

= Pythagorean theorem in an inner-product space
{parent=Inner product}

If $u$ and $v$ are orthogonal, then
$$
\lVert u+v\rVert^2=\lVert u\rVert^2+\lVert v\rVert^2.
$$

= Orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace
{parent=Inner product}

If $e_0,\ldots,e_n$ is an orthogonal basis of a finite-dimensional subspace $W$, the unique closest point in $W$ to $f$ is
$$
P_Wf=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{\langle f,e_k\rangle}{\langle e_k,e_k\rangle}e_k.
$$
The residual $f-P_Wf$ is orthogonal to $W$.

= Orthogonal projection matrix
{parent=Orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace}

A complex matrix $P$ represents an <orthogonal projection> exactly when
$$
P^2=P=P^\dagger.
$$
Its image and kernel are orthogonal complements.

= Transverse projection operator
{parent=Orthogonal projection matrix}

For a nonnull vector $k$ in a space with a nondegenerate bilinear form, $P_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-k_\mu k_\nu/k^2$ projects onto the subspace transverse to $k$. It obeys $P_{\mu\nu}k^\nu=0$ and $P^2=P$.

= Least-squares polynomial in an orthogonal-polynomial basis
{parent=Orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace}

For orthogonal polynomials $Q_0,\ldots,Q_n$ under a positive weighted integral inner product, the least-squares approximation to $f$ of degree at most $n$ is
$$
p_n^*=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{\langle f,Q_k\rangle}{\langle Q_k,Q_k\rangle}Q_k.
$$
Its residual is orthogonal to every polynomial of degree at most $n$.

= Orthogonal vectors
{parent=Inner product}

= Orthogonal
{synonym}

= Orthogonality
{synonym}

Two <vectors> are orthogonal when their <inner product> is zero.

= Nonorthogonal vectors
{parent=Inner product}

= Nonorthogonal
{synonym}

Two <vectors> are nonorthogonal when their <inner product> is nonzero. In the <power method>, the starting vector must be nonorthogonal to a dominant <eigenvector>.

= Gram-Schmidt process
{parent=Inner product}
{c}
{wiki}

The Gram-Schmidt process replaces linearly independent vectors $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ by orthogonal vectors with the same successive spans, subtracting from each $v_k$ its projections onto the preceding vectors.

= QR decomposition
{title2=$A=QR$}
{c}
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}
{wiki=QR_decomposition}

A reduced QR decomposition of a full-column-rank matrix writes $A=QR$, where $Q$ has orthonormal columns and $R$ is upper triangular with positive diagonal.

= Orthogonal coordinate reduction of a subspace
{parent=QR decomposition}

For a full-column-rank $n\times k$ matrix $V$, extend the thin <QR decomposition> $V=Q_1R_1$ to an <orthogonal matrix> $Q=(Q_1\ Q_2)$. Then
$$
Q^TV=\begin{pmatrix}R_1\\0\end{pmatrix},
$$
so $Q^T$ maps the column space of $V$ onto the first $k$ coordinate directions. Successive <Householder transformations> construct the same reduction without first forming a full orthogonal basis.

= Linear least-squares problem
{title2=$\min_x\|Ax-b\|_2$}
{parent=QR decomposition}

A linear least-squares problem minimizes $\|Ax-b\|_2$ over $x$. If $A=QR$ has full column rank, orthogonality reduces it to the upper-triangular system formed from the leading block of $R$ and the leading entries of $Q^Tb$.

= Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization for a symmetric bilinear form
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}

For a symmetric bilinear form $B$, the same projection formula
$$
u_k=v_k-\sum_{j<k}\frac{B(v_k,u_j)}{B(u_j,u_j)}u_j
$$
produces a $B$-orthogonal basis whenever the chosen pivots $B(u_j,u_j)$ are nonzero. Reordering the input vectors can avoid a zero pivot when a suitable anisotropic vector remains.

= Quadratic form
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A quadratic form is a homogeneous degree-two function $Q(v)$ represented in coordinates by $v^TAv$ with $A$ symmetric.

= Positive-definite quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A real quadratic form $q$ is positive definite when $q(v)>0$ for every nonzero vector $v$. In a basis that diagonalizes it, this is equivalent to every diagonal coefficient being positive.

= Ternary quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A ternary quadratic form is a <quadratic form> in three variables. A diagonal integral example is $aX^2+bY^2+cZ^2$ with integer coefficients $a,b,c$.

= Hyperbolic plane (quadratic form)
{parent=Quadratic form}

A hyperbolic plane is a two-dimensional quadratic space with a basis $e,f$ satisfying $q(e)=q(f)=0$ and $B(e,f)=1$. Its diagonal form has one positive and one negative square, so its signature is zero.

= Parallelogram law
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

The parallelogram law for a quadratic form $Q$ is
$$
Q(x+y)+Q(x-y)=2Q(x)+2Q(y).
$$
Conversely, under the usual divisibility assumptions, a function satisfying this identity and the appropriate homogeneity comes from a symmetric bilinear form by polarization.

= Rank of a quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

The rank of a quadratic form is the rank of its associated symmetric bilinear form.

= Rank-one quadratic form
{parent=Rank of a quadratic form}

= Rank
{synonym}

A rank-one quadratic form is the square of a <linear form> up to a scalar. More generally, a bilinear expression $(u^Tx)(v^Ty)$ is rank one because its coefficient matrix is the <outer product> $uv^T$.

= Signature of a quadratic form
{title2=$\sigma$}
{parent=Quadratic form}

= Signature
{synonym}

The signature of a real quadratic form is the number of positive squares minus the number of negative squares in its diagonal normal form.

= Polarization identity
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

Over a field of characteristic other than two, a quadratic form recovers its symmetric bilinear form by
$$B(u,v)=\frac12\bigl(Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v)\bigr).$$

= Polarization argument for a vanishing quadratic form
{parent=Polarization identity}

For a complex sesquilinear form $B$, the values $B(x,x)$ determine $B(x,y)$. In particular, expanding at $x+y$ and $x+iy$ shows that $B(x,x)=0$ for every $x$ implies $B=0$. Over the reals this conclusion can fail for nonsymmetric bilinear forms, as a nonzero skew-symmetric form has zero diagonal.

= Positive semidefinite bilinear form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A symmetric bilinear form is positive semidefinite when $B(v,v)\geq0$ for every $v$, and positive definite when equality is possible only for $v=0$.

= Definite matrix
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

A definite matrix is a Hermitian matrix whose associated quadratic form has a fixed sign. Positive and negative semidefinite matrices allow zero as well.

= Positive semidefinite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

A real symmetric or complex Hermitian matrix $A$ is positive semidefinite when $x^\dagger Ax\geq0$ for every <vector> $x$, equivalently when all its <eigenvalue>[eigenvalues] are nonnegative.

= Loewner order
{title2=$\preceq$}
{c}
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}
{wiki}

The Loewner order on <Hermitian matrices> is defined by $A\preceq B$ when $B-A$ is a <positive semidefinite matrix>.

= Square root of a matrix
{title2=$B^2=A$}
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}
{wiki}

A square root of a square matrix $A$ is a matrix $B$ satisfying $B^2=A$.

= Principal square root of a positive semidefinite matrix
{title2=$\sqrt A$}
{parent=Square root of a matrix}

If $A=Q\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)Q^T$ is real symmetric and positive semidefinite, its principal square root is
$$
\sqrt A=Q\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_1},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_n})Q^T.
$$
It is symmetric and positive semidefinite, and is positive definite exactly when $A$ is.

= Polar decomposition of an invertible real matrix
{parent=Principal square root of a positive semidefinite matrix}
{wiki=Polar_decomposition}

Every invertible real matrix has the polar decomposition
$$
M=RP,
\qquad
P=\sqrt{M^TM},
\qquad
R=MP^{-1},
$$
where $P$ is symmetric positive definite and $R$ is orthogonal.

= Polar decomposition of an invertible complex matrix
{parent=Polar decomposition of an invertible real matrix}

Every invertible complex matrix has the unique polar decomposition
$$
M=PU,
\qquad
P=\sqrt{MM^*},
\qquad
U=P^{-1}M,
$$
where $P$ is a positive-definite <Hermitian matrix> and $U$ is a <unitary matrix>. Writing $P=e^B$ gives $B=\frac12\log(MM^*)$, which is Hermitian.

= Positive-definite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

A real symmetric matrix $A$ is positive definite when
$$
x^TAx>0
$$
for every nonzero real <vector> $x$. It therefore defines the <inner product> $\langle x,y\rangle_A=x^TAy$ and is invertible.

= Cholesky decomposition
{c}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

Every real symmetric <positive-definite matrix> has a unique factorization $A=LL^T$ in which $L$ is a lower <triangular matrix> with positive diagonal entries.

= Rank-one Cholesky update
{c}
{parent=Cholesky decomposition}

Given a <Cholesky decomposition> $A=LL^T$, a rank-one Cholesky update computes a triangular factor of $A+xx^T$ in $O(n^2)$ operations without refactorizing the matrix from scratch.

= Sylvester's criterion
{c}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

A Hermitian matrix is positive definite exactly when all its leading principal minors are positive.

= Condition number
{title2=$\kappa$}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

The condition number of a problem measures how strongly relative perturbations of its input can amplify relative changes in its output. For an invertible matrix and a chosen operator norm, $\kappa(A)=\lVert A\rVert\lVert A^{-1}\rVert$.

= Spectral condition number of a positive-definite matrix
{title2=$\kappa_2(A)$}
{parent=Condition number}

For a real symmetric positive-definite matrix,
$$
\kappa_2(A)=\frac{\lambda_{\max}(A)}{\lambda_{\min}(A)}.
$$
It measures the sensitivity of the linear system and controls convergence bounds for iterative solvers.

= Hermitian positive-definite matrix
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}

A complex matrix $A$ is Hermitian positive definite when $A^*=A$ and $z^*Az>0$ for every nonzero complex vector $z$.

= Toeplitz matrix
{parent=Hermitian positive-definite matrix}
{c}
{wiki}

A Toeplitz matrix is constant along each diagonal, so its entries have the form $T_{nm}=t_{n-m}$.

= Symmetric tridiagonal Toeplitz matrix
{parent=Toeplitz matrix}

An $m\times m$ symmetric tridiagonal Toeplitz matrix has one constant diagonal $\alpha$ and equal constant subdiagonal and superdiagonal $\beta$. Its eigenvectors are the <discrete sine transform> vectors $q^{(k)}_j=\sin(jk\pi/(m+1))$, with eigenvalues $\alpha+2\beta\cos(k\pi/(m+1))$.

= Toeplitz antisymmetric tridiagonal matrix
{c}
{parent=Toeplitz matrix}

An $M\times M$ Toeplitz antisymmetric tridiagonal matrix with diagonal $a$, superdiagonal $b$, and subdiagonal $-b$ has eigenvalues
$$
\lambda_j=a+2ib\cos\frac{j\pi}{M+1},
\qquad 1\leq j\leq M.
$$
All such matrices share an orthonormal eigenbasis after the same diagonal unitary change from the discrete sine basis.

= Negative-definite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

A real symmetric matrix $A$ is negative definite when $x^TAx<0$ for every nonzero real <vector> $x$. Equivalently, $-A$ is a <positive-definite matrix> and every <eigenvalue> of $A$ is negative.

= Sylvester's law of inertia
{parent=Quadratic form}
{c}
{wiki=Sylvester's_law_of_inertia}

Every real quadratic form is congruent to a diagonal form with entries $+1,-1,0$, and the numbers of entries of each kind are invariant.

= Isotropic quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

An isotropic quadratic form has a nonzero vector on which it vanishes.

= Totally isotropic subspace
{parent=Isotropic quadratic form}

A subspace $E$ is totally isotropic for $B$ when $B(u,v)=0$ for all $u,v\in E$. For a real <nondegenerate bilinear form> of inertia $(p,q)$, its dimension is at most $\min(p,q)$.

= Isotropic vector
{parent=Isotropic quadratic form}

A vector $v$ is isotropic for a quadratic form $q$ when $q(v)=0$.

= Quadratic form gradient
{parent=Quadratic form}

For symmetric $A$, the quadratic form $x^TAx$ has gradient $2Ax$.

= Schur product theorem
{parent=Linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

The entrywise product of two positive semidefinite matrices is positive semidefinite. Writing each matrix as a sum of rank-one Gram matrices reduces the claim to $(uu^T)\circ(vv^T)=(u\circ v)(u\circ v)^T$.

= Orthogonal group
{title2=$O(n)$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

The real orthogonal group is $O(n)=\{R:R^TR=I\}$. Its tangent space at the identity is the vector space of skew-symmetric matrices.

= Orthogonal matrix
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

A real square matrix is orthogonal when $R^TR=I$, equivalently when its columns form an orthonormal basis.

= Orthogonal transformation
{parent=Orthogonal matrix}
{wiki}

An orthogonal transformation of a real inner-product space is a linear map $Q$ satisfying $Q^TQ=I$. It preserves inner products, lengths, angles, and therefore <Gaussian curvature> when applied to an embedded surface in Euclidean space.

= Improper orthogonal transformation
{parent=Orthogonal group}

An improper orthogonal transformation has determinant $-1$. In three dimensions it may be a plane reflection or a rotation composed with a reflection perpendicular to the rotation axis.

= Special orthogonal group
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

The special orthogonal group is
$$
SO(n)=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb R):A^TA=I,\ \det A=1\}.
$$
Every element of $SO(2)$ is a rotation in the plane. Every element of $SO(3)$ fixes an axis and restricts to a planar rotation on its perpendicular plane.

= Special orthogonal group as a submanifold
{title2=$T_RSO(n)=R\mathfrak{so}(n)$}
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

The map $A\mapsto A^TA$ from real matrices to symmetric matrices has derivative $H\mapsto R^TH+H^TR$ at $R\in O(n)$, which is surjective. Thus $SO(n)$ is a submanifold of dimension $n(n-1)/2$, and
$$
T_RSO(n)=\{H:R^TH+H^TR=0\}
=\{RA:A^T=-A\}.
$$

= Unit-vector stabilizer in a special orthogonal group
{title2=$S_v$}
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

For $v\in S^n$, the stabilizer $S_v=\{R\in SO(n+1):Rv=v\}$ is conjugate to the block subgroup $SO(n)$ acting on $v^\perp$. Hence it is a submanifold of dimension $n(n-1)/2$, with
$$
T_RS_v=\{RA:A^T=-A,\ Av=0\}.
$$

= Distinct unit-vector stabilizers are not transverse
{parent=Unit-vector stabilizer in a special orthogonal group}

For distinct $v,w\in S^n$, the submanifolds $S_v,S_w\subseteq SO(n+1)$ are not transverse. They both contain the identity. If $v,w$ are independent, the bivector $v\wedge w$ is a nonzero normal direction common to both tangent spaces there; if $w=-v$, the two stabilizers coincide.

= Rotation in three dimensions
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

A rotation in three dimensions is an element of $SO(3)$. It fixes an axis and acts as a two-dimensional rotation on the plane perpendicular to that axis.

= Skew-symmetric matrix
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

= Skew-symmetric
{synonym}

= Antisymmetric matrix
{synonym}

A matrix is skew-symmetric when $A^T=-A$.

= Cross-product matrix
{parent=Skew-symmetric matrix}

For $a=(a_1,a_2,a_3)^T$, the map $b\mapsto a\times b$ has matrix
$$
[a]_\times=
\begin{pmatrix}
0&-a_3&a_2\\
a_3&0&-a_1\\
-a_2&a_1&0
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
It has trace zero and annihilates $a$, so its determinant is zero.

= Tensor product of linear maps
{parent=Linear algebra}

The <tensor product> of linear maps is determined by $(A\otimes B)(x\otimes y)=Ax\otimes By$ and linear extension.

= Trace-duality bound
{parent=Linear algebra}

For $M\succeq0$ with $\operatorname{tr}M\leq s$, $\operatorname{tr}(MA)\leq s\max(\lambda_{\max}(A),0)\leq s\lVert A\rVert_{\rm op}$.

= Diagonal matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A diagonal matrix has zero entries away from its main diagonal. Its eigenvalues are its diagonal entries.

= Block-diagonal matrix
{parent=Diagonal matrix}

A block-diagonal matrix has square matrices along its block diagonal and zero off-diagonal blocks. Its determinant is the product of the block determinants.

= Triangular matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

An upper or lower triangular matrix has all entries on one side of the main diagonal equal to zero. Its eigenvalues, with algebraic multiplicity, are its diagonal entries.

= Upper triangular matrix
{parent=Triangular matrix}

An upper triangular matrix has zero entries below its main diagonal. Its <determinant> is the product of its diagonal entries, and an invertible upper triangular linear system is solved by backward substitution.

= Lower triangular matrix
{parent=Triangular matrix}

A lower triangular matrix has zero entries above its main diagonal. Its <determinant> is the product of its diagonal entries, and an invertible lower triangular linear system is solved by forward substitution.

= Lower triangular matrix algebra
{title2=$T_n(k)$}
{parent=Lower triangular matrix}

The algebra $T_n(k)$ consists of the lower triangular $n$ by $n$ matrices over $k$. Its <Jacobson radical> $N$ consists of the strictly lower triangular matrices, and
$$
N^i=\operatorname{span}\{e_{rs}:r-s\geq i\}.
$$
The $n$ simple left modules $S_r$ are one-dimensional, with a matrix acting through its $r$th diagonal entry. For the left regular module,
$$
N^i/N^{i+1}\cong S_{i+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus S_n.
$$

= Triangular linear system
{parent=Triangular matrix}

A triangular linear system is solved by forward substitution for a lower triangular coefficient matrix or backward substitution for an upper triangular coefficient matrix, using $O(n^2)$ operations for a dense $n$ by $n$ matrix.

= Forward substitution in a triangular system
{parent=Triangular linear system}

Forward substitution solves a lower-triangular system from its first row downward, substituting each newly determined variable into the rows below.

= Backward substitution in a triangular system
{parent=Triangular linear system}

Backward substitution solves an upper-triangular system from its last row upward.

= Square root
{title2=$\sqrt{x}$}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

A square root of an element $x$ is an element $y$ such that $y^2=x$. The notation $\sqrt{x}$ denotes the nonnegative square root when $x$ is a <real number>[nonnegative real number].

= Algebraic number
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Quadratic irrational number
{parent=Algebraic number}
{wiki}

= Quadratic irrational
{synonym}

A quadratic irrational number is a real irrational root of a quadratic polynomial with integer coefficients.

= Golden ratio
{title2=$\varphi$}
{parent=Algebraic number}
{wiki}

The golden ratio is the positive number
$$
\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2},
$$
which satisfies $\varphi^2=\varphi+1$ and $1/\varphi=\varphi-1$.

= Countability of algebraic numbers
{parent=Algebraic number}

Integer polynomials form a countable set and each has finitely many roots, so the algebraic numbers are countable.

= Bezout identity
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

= Eigenfunction expansion
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Field extension
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Degree of a field extension
{title2=$[L:K]$}
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki}

The degree $[L:K]$ of a field extension is the <dimension of a vector space> $L$ over $K$.

= Algebraically independent elements
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki=Algebraic_independence}

Elements $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ of a field extension of $K$ are algebraically independent over $K$ when no nonzero polynomial in $K[X_1,\ldots,X_n]$ vanishes at $(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$.

= Rational function field
{title2=$K(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$}
{parent=Algebraically independent elements}
{wiki}

The rational function field $K(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ is the field of fractions of $K[x_1,\ldots,x_n]$ when the variables are algebraically independent over $K$.

= Adjoining a root of an irreducible polynomial
{parent=Field extension}

If $f\in k[X]$ is irreducible, then $(f)$ is a maximal ideal and
$$
k[X]/(f)
$$
is a field extension of $k$. The class $\alpha=X+(f)$ is a root of $f$, so this quotient adjoins a root of $f$ to the base field.

= Tower law
{parent=Field extension}

For finite extensions $K\subseteq L\subseteq M$,
$$
[M:K]=[M:L][L:K].
$$

= Field compositum
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki=Compositum_(field_theory)}

Inside a common overfield, the compositum $L_1L_2$ is the smallest field containing both $L_1$ and $L_2$.

= Linearly disjoint field extensions
{parent=Field compositum}
{wiki=Linearly_disjoint}

Finite extensions $L_1/K$ and $L_2/K$ are linearly disjoint when products of basis elements form a basis of their compositum. In particular,
$$
[L_1L_2:K]=[L_1:K][L_2:K].
$$
Extensions of coprime finite degrees are linearly disjoint.

= Algebraic closure
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki}

An algebraic closure of a field $K$ is an algebraic extension $\overline K/K$ for which $\overline K$ is algebraically closed.

= Intermediate field
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki}

For a field extension $L/K$, an intermediate field is a field $M$ satisfying $K\subseteq M\subseteq L$.

= Quadratic extension
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki}

A quadratic extension is a field extension of degree two. It is normal because the other root of the minimal polynomial $T^2+bT+c$ of any non-base-field element is $-b-\alpha$ and lies in the same extension.

= Transitivity of algebraic extensions
{parent=Field extension}
{wiki=Algebraic_extension}

If $L/K$ and $K/F$ are algebraic field extensions, then $L/F$ is algebraic. In particular, an element algebraic over an algebraic extension of $F$ is algebraic over $F$.

= Finite field
{title2=$\mathbb F_q$}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

A finite field is a field with finitely many elements; its size is a prime power.

= Polynomial characterization of a finite field
{title2=$X^{q^n}-X$}
{parent=Finite field}

In a splitting field over $\mathbb F_q$, the polynomial $X^{q^n}-X$ has $q^n$ distinct roots because its derivative is $-1$. Its roots are closed under addition, multiplication, additive inverses, and nonzero inverses, so they form the field $\mathbb F_{q^n}$. The splitting field consequently has degree $n$ over $\mathbb F_q$.

= Irreducible factors of a finite-field Frobenius polynomial
{title2=$X^{q^n}-X$}
{parent=Polynomial characterization of a finite field}

Every monic irreducible polynomial over $\mathbb F_q$ whose degree divides $n$ divides $X^{q^n}-X$. In particular, if $f$ has degree $n$ and $\alpha$ is one of its roots, then $\mathbb F_q(\alpha)$ has $q^n$ elements, so $\alpha^{q^n}=\alpha$ and the minimal polynomial $f$ divides $X^{q^n}-X$.

= Number of monic irreducible polynomials over a finite field
{title2=$N_q(n)$}
{parent=Irreducible factors of a finite-field Frobenius polynomial}

Let $N_q(n)$ be the number of <monic> <irreducible polynomials> of <polynomial degree> $n$ over $\mathbb F_q$. Factoring $X^{q^n}-X$ into all such polynomials whose degrees divide $n$ gives
$$
q^n=\sum_{d\mid n}dN_q(d).
$$
The <Möbius inversion formula> therefore gives
$$
N_q(n)=\frac1n\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)q^{n/d}.
$$

= Finite field extension
{parent=Finite field}

Every degree-$n$ extension of $\mathbb F_q$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb F_{q^n}$.

= Finite-field Frobenius automorphism
{title2=$x\mapsto x^q$}
{parent=Finite field extension}

The map $x\mapsto x^q$ is an automorphism of $\mathbb F_{q^n}$ fixing $\mathbb F_q$. Its powers are all the $\mathbb F_q$-automorphisms, and its order is $n$.

= Galois group of a finite field extension
{title2=$\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb F_{q^n}/\mathbb F_q)$}
{c}
{parent=Finite-field Frobenius automorphism}

Every <finite field extension> is a <Galois extension>, and
$$
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb F_{q^n}/\mathbb F_q)
=\langle x\mapsto x^q\rangle\cong C_n.
$$

= Multiplicative group of a finite field
{parent=Finite field}

The multiplicative group of a finite field $\mathbb F_q$ is the group $\mathbb F_q^\times$ of its $q-1$ nonzero elements under multiplication.

= Multiplicative group of a finite field is cyclic
{parent=Multiplicative group of a finite field}

The nonzero elements of every finite field form a cyclic group. For a field with $q$ elements, combine elements realizing each prime-power factor of the group exponent to obtain an element of exponent order; the root bound for $X^m-1$ then forces that order to be $q-1$.

= Finite multiplicative subgroup of a field is cyclic
{parent=Multiplicative group of a finite field is cyclic}

Every finite <subgroup> $H$ of the multiplicative group of a <field> is <cyclic group>[cyclic]. If $m$ is the exponent of $H$, commutativity lets one combine elements whose orders realize the prime-power factors of $m$, producing an element of order $m$. Every element of $H$ is a root of $X^m-1$, so the <Lagrange root bound over a field> gives $|H|\leq m$. Since $m\leq|H|$, equality holds and that element generates $H$.

= Power roots in a finite field
{parent=Multiplicative group of a finite field is cyclic}

In a finite field with $q$ elements, the equation $x^k=1$ has exactly $\gcd(k,q-1)$ solutions. Indeed, after choosing a generator $g$ of the multiplicative group, $x=g^j$ is a solution exactly when $(q-1)\mid kj$.

= Index-two square-class argument for three related residues
{parent=Multiplicative group of a finite field is cyclic}

For odd $p$, the nonzero squares form an index-two subgroup of $\mathbb F_p^\times$. Given nonzero $a,b$, either $a$ or $b$ is a square, or both occupy the nontrivial coset and $ab$ is a square. Taking $a=2$ and $b=3$ shows that one of $2,3,6$ is a square, apart from the immediate zero case $p=3$.

= Function composition
{title2=$\circ$}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Injective function
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Injective
{synonym}

= Two-to-one function
{parent=Algebra}

A function is two-to-one when every element of its image has exactly two preimages.

= Irrational number
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Irrotational vector field
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Linear recurrence relation
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Characteristic equation of a linear recurrence
{parent=Linear recurrence relation}

For a constant-coefficient <linear recurrence relation>, substituting a trial sequence $u_n=r^n$ produces a polynomial equation in $r$ called its characteristic equation. Repeated roots contribute factors polynomial in $n$.

= Ordinary generating function of a recurrence
{parent=Linear recurrence relation}

For a sequence $(u_n)$, the ordinary generating function is
$$
G(x)=\sum_{n\geq0}u_nx^n.
$$
Multiplying a linear recurrence by $x^n$ and summing converts shifts of the sequence into polynomial multiples of $G$.

= Floor-half generating function
{parent=Ordinary generating function of a recurrence}

The sequence $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ has generating function
$$
\sum_{n\geq1}\left\lfloor\frac n2\right\rfloor x^n
=\frac{x^2}{(1-x)^2(1+x)}.
$$

= Affine recurrence
{parent=Linear recurrence relation}

An affine recurrence has the form $x_{n+1}=Ax_n+b$ and is solved by a matrix geometric sum.

= Stable affine recurrence
{parent=Affine recurrence}

If the spectral radius of $A$ is below one, $x_{n+1}=Ax_n+b$ converges to the unique fixed point $(I-A)^{-1}b$.

= Linearization
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

Linearization replaces a nonlinear relation near a reference state by its first-order approximation.

= Linear order
{parent=Linearization}

Working to linear order in a small parameter retains constant and first-degree terms and discards all terms of quadratic and higher order.

= Linearization of a dynamical system
{parent=Linearization}
{wiki}

= Mathematical induction
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Strong induction
{parent=Mathematical induction}
{wiki}

Strong induction allows the step for an integer to use the result for every smaller integer.

= Backward induction
{parent=Mathematical induction}
{wiki}

Backward induction proves or computes a finite sequence of statements by starting at its terminal index and stepping toward the initial index.

= Minimal number of generators
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Nilpotent polynomial
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Noetherian ring
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

= Noetherian
{c}
{synonym}

= Noetherian module
{c}
{parent=Noetherian ring}
{wiki}

An $R$-module is Noetherian when every <submodule> is finitely generated, equivalently when every ascending chain of submodules stabilizes. Quotients and finite direct sums of Noetherian modules are Noetherian.

= Noetherian local ring
{c}
{parent=Noetherian ring}

A Noetherian local ring is a Noetherian ring with a unique maximal ideal.

= Ideal of definition
{parent=Noetherian local ring}

An ideal $I$ of a <Noetherian local ring> $(A,\mathfrak m)$ is an ideal of definition when $A/I$ has <Krull dimension> zero. Equivalently, $I$ is <primary ideal>[$\mathfrak m$-primary], so $\sqrt I=\mathfrak m$ and $\mathfrak m^r\subseteq I$ for some <positive integer> $r$.

= Ascending chain condition
{parent=Noetherian ring}
{wiki=Ascending_chain_condition}

The ascending chain condition requires every chain
$$
I_1\subseteq I_2\subseteq\cdots
$$
of ideals to stabilize. A ring is Noetherian exactly when it satisfies this condition, equivalently when every ideal is finitely generated.

= Noetherianity from finite additive rank
{parent=Noetherian ring}

If a ring $R$ is finitely generated as an abelian group, then it is Noetherian. Every ideal is an additive subgroup of that finitely generated abelian group and is therefore generated by finitely many elements as an abelian group. The same elements generate it as an $R$-ideal because integer multiples are already scalar multiples by elements of $R$.

= Hilbert basis theorem
{parent=Noetherian ring}
{c}
{wiki}

A polynomial ring over a Noetherian ring is Noetherian.

= Noetherianity of a quadratic integer order
{parent=Noetherian ring}

For a nonsquare integer $d$,
$$
\mathbb Z[\sqrt d]\cong\mathbb Z[X]/(X^2-d).
$$
The integers are Noetherian, the Hilbert basis theorem makes $\mathbb Z[X]$ Noetherian, and quotients preserve Noetherianity.

= Nonprincipal ideal in the integers adjoined a square root of minus five
{parent=Noetherianity of a quadratic integer order}

In $R=\mathbb Z[\sqrt{-5}]$, the ideal
$$
I=(2,1+\sqrt{-5})
$$
has additive index two. If $I=(a+b\sqrt{-5})$ were principal, multiplication by its generator would give
$$
[R:I]=|a^2+5b^2|=2,
$$
which has no solution in integers. Thus $I$ is not principal.

= Constant-plus-ideal non-Noetherian subring
{parent=Noetherian ring}

The subring $R=K+XYK[X,Y]$ is not Noetherian. Its ideals
$$
(XY)_R\subsetneq(XY,XY^2)_R\subsetneq\cdots
$$
form a strict ascending chain because multiplying by a nonconstant element of $R$ introduces at least a second power of $X$.

= Artinian ring
{c}
{parent=Noetherian ring}
{wiki}

= Artinian
{c}
{synonym}

An Artinian ring satisfies the descending chain condition on ideals. A commutative ring is Artinian exactly when it is Noetherian and has <Krull dimension> zero.

= Prime ideals of a commutative Artinian ring
{parent=Artinian ring}

A commutative <Artinian ring> has finitely many <prime ideals>, and every one is a <maximal ideal>. If $\mathfrak p$ is prime, the Artinian domain $R/\mathfrak p$ is a field: stabilization of $(x)\supseteq(x^2)\supseteq\cdots$ gives $x^n=x^{n+1}a$, and cancellation gives $xa=1$. Infinitely many distinct maximal ideals $\mathfrak m_i$ would give a strictly descending chain
$$
\mathfrak m_1\supsetneq\mathfrak m_1\mathfrak m_2\supsetneq
\mathfrak m_1\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_3\supsetneq\cdots,
$$
because distinct maximal ideals are comaximal.

= Nilradical of a commutative Artinian ring
{parent=Artinian ring}

The <nilradical> $N$ of a commutative <Artinian ring> is a <nilpotent ideal>. Its powers stabilize, say $N^r=N^{r+1}=I$. If $I\ne0$, choose an ideal $J$ minimal subject to $IJ\ne0$, and then $x\in J$ with $Ix\ne0$. Minimality and $I^2=I$ give $Rx=Ix$, so $x=ax$ for some $a\in I$. Since $a$ is nilpotent, $1-a$ is a <unit>, contradicting $(1-a)x=0$ and $x\ne0$.

= Artinian commutative ring is Noetherian theorem
{c}
{parent=Artinian ring}

Every commutative <Artinian ring> is <Noetherian>. Its <nilradical> $N$ is nilpotent by the <nilradical of a commutative Artinian ring>, and the <Chinese remainder theorem> identifies the reduced quotient $R/N$ with the finite product of the residue fields at the finitely many <maximal ideals>. Each $N^i/N^{i+1}$ is an Artinian module over that product of <fields> and hence a <finite-dimensional vector space>. The finite <filtration> by these layers makes every <ideal> finitely generated.

= Artinian local ring
{c}
{parent=Artinian ring}

An Artinian local ring is an Artinian ring with a unique maximal ideal. Its maximal ideal is nilpotent.

= Principal ideals in an Artinian local ring with one-dimensional cotangent space
{parent=Artinian local ring}

Let $(R,\mathfrak m)$ be an <Artinian local ring>. If $\dim_{R/\mathfrak m}\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2=1$, then every ideal of $R$ is principal. The <Nakayama lemma> gives $\mathfrak m=(x)$, and nilpotence gives a finite chain
$$
R\supset(x)\supset(x^2)\supset\cdots\supset0.
$$
For a nonzero ideal $J$, choose the largest $r$ for which $J\subseteq(x^r)$ and take $y\in J\setminus(x^{r+1})$. Writing $y=x^ru$, one has $u\notin\mathfrak m$, so $u$ is a unit and $J=(x^r)=(y)$.

= Finiteness criterion for an Artinian local ring
{c}
{parent=Artinian local ring}

An Artinian local ring is a finite set exactly when its residue field is finite. If $\mathfrak m^r=0$, each layer $\mathfrak m^i/\mathfrak m^{i+1}$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over that residue field.

= Noetherian dimension-zero criterion for an Artinian ring
{c}
{parent=Artinian ring}

A commutative Noetherian ring is Artinian if and only if every prime ideal is maximal. Equivalently, it is Artinian exactly when its Krull dimension is zero.

= Pauli matrix
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki=Pauli_matrices}

The Pauli matrices $\sigma_x,\sigma_y,\sigma_z$ are traceless Hermitian $2\times2$ matrices that, together with the identity, form a basis of the complex $2\times2$ matrices.

= Pauli matrix multiplication law
{c}
{parent=Pauli matrix}

The Pauli matrices satisfy
$$
\sigma_i\sigma_j=\delta_{ij}I+i\varepsilon_{ijk}\sigma_k.
$$
Consequently each squares to the identity, distinct Pauli matrices anticommute, and their products span $M_2(\mathbb C)$.

= Pauli matrix commutator identity
{c}
{parent=Pauli matrix multiplication law}

For vectors $\mathbf a,\mathbf b\in\mathbb R^3$, the <Pauli matrix multiplication law> gives
$$
[\mathbf a\mathbin\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma,\mathbf b\mathbin\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma]
=2i(\mathbf a\times\mathbf b)\mathbin\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma.
$$

= Gell-Mann matrix
{c}
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki=Gell-Mann_matrices}

The eight Gell-Mann matrices are traceless Hermitian $3\times3$ matrices forming a conventional basis for the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{su}(3)$ after multiplication by $i$.

= Pigeonhole principle
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Infinite pigeonhole principle
{parent=Pigeonhole principle}

If an infinite set is partitioned into finitely many parts, at least one part is infinite.

= Polynomial interpolation error
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Hermite interpolation
{parent=Polynomial interpolation error}
{c}
{wiki}

Hermite interpolation prescribes values and finitely many derivatives of a polynomial at distinct points.

= RSA cryptosystem
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

For $N=pq$, choose $e$ coprime to $\phi(N)$ and a private exponent $d$ with $ed\equiv1\pmod{\phi(N)}$. Encryption and decryption are
$$
c\equiv m^e\pmod N,
\qquad
m\equiv c^d\pmod N.
$$

= Common-modulus RSA attack
{parent=RSA cryptosystem}

If the same invertible message $m$ is sent under one RSA modulus with coprime public exponents $e_1,e_2$, choose integers $a,b$ with $ae_1+be_2=1$. From $c_i=m^{e_i}\pmod N$ one recovers
$$
m\equiv c_1^ac_2^b\pmod N,
$$
using modular inverses for negative exponents.

= RSA private exponent reveals the factorization
{parent=RSA cryptosystem}
{c}

Knowledge of a valid public and private exponent gives $ed-1$, a nonzero multiple of $\lambda(N)$. Writing it as $2^st$ with $t$ odd and testing random bases produces a nontrivial square root of one modulo $N$ with high probability; a greatest common divisor then factors $N$. Thus one customer's private key under a shared semiprime modulus compromises every exponent using that modulus.

= Complementary RSA fixed points
{c}
{parent=RSA cryptosystem}

An RSA fixed point satisfies $m^e\equiv m\pmod N$. Since every valid public exponent $e$ is odd, $(-m)^e\equiv-m^e\equiv-m\pmod N$; therefore $N-m$ is a fixed point whenever $m$ is.

= Root of unity
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Roots of unity
{synonym}

An $N$th root of unity is a <complex number> $z$ satisfying $z^N=1$. The primitive root $e^{2\pi i/N}$ generates all $N$th roots.

= Primitive root of unity
{parent=Root of unity}

A primitive $n$th root of unity has multiplicative order exactly $n$.

= Orthogonality of roots of unity
{parent=Root of unity}

For $\omega=e^{2\pi i/N}$,
$$
\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\omega^{kn}
=\begin{cases}N,&N\mid k,\\0,&N\nmid k.\end{cases}
$$
This finite geometric sum is the orthogonality relation underlying the <discrete Fourier transform>.

= Root-of-unity filter
{parent=Root of unity}

For $\omega=e^{2\pi i/N}$,
$$
\sum_{y=0}^{N-1}\omega^{my}
=\begin{cases}N,&N\mid m,\\0,&N\nmid m.\end{cases}
$$
This is the finite <geometric series> identity and expresses orthogonality of the characters of $\mathbb Z_N$.

= Smith normal form
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

For every matrix $A$ over a <principal ideal domain>, there are invertible matrices $U,V$ such that
$$
UAV=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_r,0,\ldots,0),
\qquad d_1\mid d_2\mid\cdots\mid d_r.
$$
The nonzero diagonal entries are unique up to multiplication by units. Over the <integer>[integers], $U$ and $V$ are unimodular and the $d_i$ may be chosen positive; their products $d_1\cdots d_k$ are the greatest common divisors of the $k$-rowed minors.

= Star-shaped set
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Surjective function
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Surjection
{synonym}

= Transcendental number
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Hermite theorem on the transcendence of e
{title2=$e\notin\overline{\mathbb Q}$}
{parent=Transcendental number}
{c}

The Hermite theorem states that the base $e$ of the natural logarithm is a <transcendental number>.

= Liouville number
{parent=Transcendental number}
{c}
{wiki}

A Liouville number admits rational approximations of every fixed polynomial order; Liouville's approximation theorem makes every such irrational number transcendental.

= Unique factorization domain
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Minimal-prime criterion for a Noetherian unique factorization domain
{c}
{parent=Unique factorization domain}

A Noetherian integral domain is a unique factorization domain exactly when every prime ideal minimal among the nonzero prime ideals is principal. The converse uses the Krull principal ideal theorem to prove that every irreducible element generates a prime ideal.

= Height-one prime in a unique factorization domain
{parent=Unique factorization domain}

Every height-one prime ideal in a unique factorization domain is principal and generated by an irreducible element. Consequently, two elements contained in the same height-one prime have a common irreducible factor.

= Nagata criterion for unique factorization domains
{c}
{parent=Unique factorization domain}

Let $A$ be an <integral domain> that is a <Noetherian ring>, and let $x\in A$ generate a <prime ideal>. If the <localization of a ring> $A_x$ is a <unique factorization domain>, then $A$ is a unique factorization domain. Indeed, integrality of $a/x^n$ over $A$, with $n$ minimal, forces $x\mid a$ and hence proves that $A$ is an <integrally closed domain>. The <Nagata theorem for divisor class groups> then says that $\operatorname{Cl}(A)$ is generated by the height-one primes containing $x$; the only one is the principal prime $(x)$, so $\operatorname{Cl}(A)=0$.

= Unit in a ring
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

= Unit
{synonym}

A unit in a ring is an element with a multiplicative inverse.

= Nonunit
{parent=Unit in a ring}

A nonunit is an element of a ring that has no multiplicative inverse in that ring.

= Unit group
{title2=$R^\times$}
{parent=Unit in a ring}

The units of a ring $R$ form a group $R^\times$ under multiplication.

= Multiplicative group of integers modulo n
{title2=$(\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z)^\times$}
{parent=Unit group}

The multiplicative group of integers modulo $n$ consists of the residue classes coprime to $n$. It has order $\varphi(n)$.

= Wilson theorem
{parent=Algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

For a prime $p$,
$$
(p-1)!\equiv-1\pmod p.
$$
Every nonzero residue other than $1$ and $-1$ pairs with a distinct multiplicative inverse, leaving only $1(-1)$ in the product.

= Fundamental theorem of algebra
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

Every nonconstant complex polynomial has a complex root and therefore factors completely into linear factors over $\mathbb C$.

= Group theory
{parent=Algebra}
{wiki}

Group theory studies groups, homomorphisms, actions, quotients, and symmetry.

= Group
{parent=Group theory}
{wiki=Group_(mathematics)}
